The QA app's data and WebView profile were removed afterwards; the running Agent Studio installation was not touched. Gemini, WSL hosts, a relay-routed choice from the Viewer and a mixed-release relay were covered by unit and browser tests only. Evidence: `artifacts/folder-icons-native.json`, `folder-icons-browser.png`, `folder-icons-{verify,rust,e2e-1,e2e-2}.log`.

## A WSL Codex profile borrows its Windows login too — 2026-10-02

Codex accounts added to a WSL distribution still asked for a sign-in there. Codex's app-server supports a login its host manages: `account/login/start` with `chatgptAuthTokens` signs it in with a ChatGPT access token, and the server request `account/chatgptAuthTokens/refresh` asks the host for a fresh one when a request is refused. The Windows Codex hands its own login over through `getAuthStatus` with `includeToken`, and renews it first with `refreshToken`. So a separate Codex profile in a WSL distribution now borrows its account's Windows login as well (`profiles::lender_id`). Each of its app-servers that needs the account signs in right after initializing: chats, model lists, app mentions, usage reads and reset credits. The token comes from the Windows Codex (`lending::codex_login`), cached until it nears expiry, with the account taken from the token's own claims. A chat answers refresh requests with a token the Windows Codex renews, so it stays the only renewer. Titles for borrowing connections are written on Windows, since a one-shot `codex exec` cannot take a host-managed login. Status, sign-in and re-adding follow the Windows login as for Claude.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,207 files), 581 unit/HTTP tests in 77 files, production build, 324 browser scenarios (two shards of 162), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 387 Rust tests with 20 opt-in cases ignored. Real check with Codex 0.160.0 on both sides, in a throwaway WSL Codex home, without a model turn. The Windows Codex of a separate profile returned its ChatGPT token (about six days left). The WSL app-server reported no account until `account/login/start` with that token. It then reported the ChatGPT account and plan and read the account's rate limits, and wrote no `auth.json` in WSL. New coverage: lent Codex logins are only ChatGPT logins with an account in their token, Codex profiles resolve a lender like Claude's, and the frontend names Codex lenders. Not exercised: a refresh request from a running WSL chat (the probe's token was days from expiry), and a native app build.

## A WSL Claude profile borrows its Windows login — 2026-10-02

The user asked to align WSL with the Claude app, which needs no sign-in inside WSL, and to handle the renewals. They had never agreed to the rule that kept every login inside its own CLI. The Claude app's bundle (`app.asar`, 2.19675) shows its approach: it owns the login, installs its own Claude Code in `~/.claude/remote/ccd-cli`, and passes it an access token that the app refreshes. Agent Studio's logins belong to Windows CLI profiles, so it lends them instead. Before a Claude CLI starts in a separate WSL profile whose account has a Windows connection (`profiles::lender_id`), the host writes that profile's `.credentials.json` through `wsl-lend.sh`. The file holds the Windows login's access token, expiry, scopes and plan, never the refresh token or MCP sign-ins, and is written with mode 0600 and an atomic rename. The Windows CLI stays the only renewer. A watch per login (`lending.rs`) has it renew in the login's last five minutes, when Claude Code agrees to renew on a usage read, then copies the new token to every borrowing profile. Claude Code checks its credentials file's modification time before each renewal check and rereads it when it changed. So running and parked WSL processes switch to the new token without restarting. A borrowing connection reports the Windows login's state and identity, Open sign-in signs in on Windows, and re-adding the account to WSL opens no sign-in. Codex profiles in WSL still sign in there.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,207 files), 581 unit/HTTP tests in 77 files, production build, 324 browser scenarios (two shards of 162), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 386 Rust tests with 20 opt-in cases ignored. Real checks against Claude Code 2.1.287 in the user's Ubuntu, in throwaway profiles. A probe wrote a lent login, and `claude auth status` reported it signed in through claude.ai. A stream-json session started on an expiring invalid token answered "OK" once the real token was written to its file mid-session. The same session without the swap failed with "401 OAuth access token is invalid". The opt-in `lending::tests::a_wsl_profile_reports_the_lent_login_as_signed_in` passed through the real `wsl-lend.sh` (mode 600, signed in). New coverage: the lent file keeps only the token, expiry, scopes and plan; the watch wakes inside the CLI's renewal window and backs off when a login cannot be renewed; lenders are named by connection id and only for separate Claude profiles whose account Windows has. The WSL setup browser scenario now sees the Desktop account join Ubuntu connected despite Ubuntu's own signed-out login. It re-adds the account without a sign-in, and after the Windows login signs out, sees Ubuntu ask for the Windows sign-in. Not exercised: a natural expiry renewed by the watch (a token lasts about eight hours), and a native app build.

## Desktop accounts join a WSL distribution by themselves — 2026-10-02

The user asked whether **Add 4 accounts here** was needed at all, or could be automated. The button on a WSL computer's agent card added the Desktop's accounts of that agent as separate profiles there, and stayed on screen while any were missing, including ones removed on purpose. Adding them needs no decision, so each refresh on the Windows computer now does it (`addHostAccounts`). It adds the agent's separate profiles there, and its terminal login while signed in, with accounts added on Desktop later joining too. Each still signs in once inside the distribution. A login never moves between computers, since Agent Studio never reads or copies CLI credentials, which the user accepted when WSL folders moved into their distribution on 2026-10-01.

Desktop remembers every account each of its distributions has had in device-local `preferences.distributionAccounts`, which is saved with the workspace file but never synced. So an account removed there is never added again, and **Manage account** on Desktop offers **Connect on WSL · Ubuntu** to bring it back on request. The distribution's own terminal login no longer joins beside the Desktop accounts. It joins only while Desktop has no account of that agent, so removing every account there brings nothing back. While the distribution has no account of the agent, its terminal login still joins as the Desktop account it reports, without another sign-in. An account another connection there already reports is not added twice. A refresh now checks Desktop's own new logins before its distributions take them, because an account's check reads its saved connection. A WSL computer no longer gets speculative logins for CLIs it lacks. So a new chat there with no account of its agent says that agent needs to be set up, with Open Connections, instead of borrowing Desktop's CLI status.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,207 files), 580 unit/HTTP tests in 77 files, production build, 324 browser scenarios (two shards of 162), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 383 Rust tests with 19 opt-in cases ignored. New coverage: unit tests add the Desktop accounts once (a removed one stays out, a later Desktop account joins, a signed-out Desktop login waits until it signs in, and with every account removed nothing returns, not even the distribution's own login), let a distribution login that reports a Desktop account join as that account, and leave out an account another connection there already reports. The WSL setup browser scenario installs Claude, sees the Desktop account join with its sign-in hint and no button, opens its sign-in, removes it, refreshes with account checks held so the refresh is seen to finish, sees it stay removed, and brings it back with **Connect on WSL · Ubuntu**. Three scenarios that counted the WSL computers' own logins now expect the Desktop accounts there: new accounts joining Ubuntu, Codex work joining both distributions, and Ubuntu's Codex loading its own catalog. The fix was not exercised in a native build or a real distribution; the user's Ubuntu gets its Desktop accounts on the first refresh after updating.

## A removed terminal login stays removed — 2026-10-02

The user reported that **Claude CLI login** kept coming back after they had removed it. Their saved workspace (v0.7.0) held four separate Claude profiles on Desktop beside a Windows **Claude CLI login** connection whose ID differed from the one in its 2026-09-28 backup, and two more **Claude CLI login** labels without connections. No reply had ever used it. Detection skipped a terminal login only when it reported the same account as another connection there, or when one of them was that login turned into a separate profile. The terminal's own Claude login was signed out (still so on 2026-10-01), so it reported no account, and every refresh, new chat or folder choice registered it again under a label a removed connection had left. A unit test and a browser scenario encoded that as intended, as the identity-aware detection entry of 2026-09-21 below did for a terminal signed into another account.

Detection now adds an environment's terminal login only while no account of that agent is connected there. Once one is (the login itself, a separate profile, or the login turned into one), a removed login stays removed whatever the terminal reports later, and **Add account** connects any other account. A computer's first account of an agent is still its terminal login, reusing a label a removed login left behind, and a WSL terminal login that matches an account connected elsewhere on the computer still joins that account's label. Managed WSL logins are probed only where detection can still add one. Nothing is removed automatically, so the connection already re-added stays until it is removed once more. No saved field changed, so the relay needs no update for this.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,207 files), 579 unit/HTTP tests in 77 files, production build, 324 browser scenarios (two shards of 162), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 383 Rust tests with 19 opt-in cases ignored. New coverage: the unit rule keeps a removed login out whether the terminal is unchecked, reports the same account, is signed out or reports another account, returns it under its old label once no account of the agent is left there, and keeps it out beside a converted profile with or without its marker. The browser scenario now refreshes Connections with the terminal signed out while the separate profile stays signed in, then with the terminal signed into another account, holding account checks so each refresh is seen to finish before asserting. Both the unit test and the browser scenario fail against the previous rule. The fix was not exercised in a native build; detection runs entirely in the frontend.

## A reply stopped at a usage limit keeps its history open and shows the limit as a card — 2026-09-30

The user asked for a reply that reaches its usage limit to keep its Work history open and show the limit as a card of its own, instead of a separate message. Their saved workspace held nine such Claude replies. Each had collapsed into **Work history · Failed** under a heading reading `<synthetic>`, with Claude Code's line (**You've hit your session limit · resets 1:50pm (America/Sao_Paulo)**) standing in for the answer above the generic **The provider CLI could not complete the request** card. The line was the last progress comment of a message Claude Code writes itself, whose id is a UUID rather than an API `msg_` id, and the reply's usage reported model `<synthetic>` with `contextInput: 0`, so the context meter read zero afterwards. A reply waiting for background work had stored the same line three times. In Claude Code 2.1.285 the binary answers a refused request with `Vo({content, error: "rate_limit", quotaLimits})`, a message with `isApiErrorMessage` and model `rl = "<synthetic>"`, and its stream-json writer adds `error` and `is_api_error_message` to parent assistant lines. Its limit lines start with the openings in its `A2r` list. `codex app-server generate-ts` for Codex 0.159.2 shows `CodexErrorInfo` `"usageLimitExceeded"` on `TurnError`, sent in the `error` notification and in a failed turn.

`src-tauri/src/protocol/usage_limit.rs` recognizes both and the decoder sends a revisioned `usagelimit` event instead of progress or text. Claude Code's own messages no longer set the reply's model or context, a Claude result that only repeats the line fails the reply, and a Codex turn stopped at a limit keeps its commentary out of the answer. The runner and the Codex adapter fail the reply with the provider's line. The page saves it as optional `usageLimit` (`src/lib/usage-limits.ts`), merged by revision in `sameRun` and kept by `retainQuestions` when an older relay drops it, and never replayed. `MessageView` shows a warning card in place of the error card and no stand-in answer, and `ToolActivity` keeps Work history open, labelled **Limit reached**. The card adds a reset hint only for lines that name a reset and offers **Switch account**, which opens the Agent picker, for the latest reply of an idle chat with another account of its agent. Replies saved earlier are recognized by their `<synthetic>` model and Claude's wording, and their headings and context meter ignore that model. `relay/Dockerfile` copies the new module.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,200 files), 566 unit/HTTP tests in 75 files, production build, 318 browser scenarios (two shards of 159), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 358 Rust tests with 13 opt-in cases ignored. New coverage: decoder tests for both providers (including sub-agent, retrying, warning and 429 lines, repeated limits and results without `is_error`), a runner test driving the PowerShell CLI fixture through Claude Code's exact line sequence, unit tests for the record, merges, prompt exclusion, saved replies, model name and context, and `tests/usage-limits.spec.ts` (the card while running, the open history after failure, folding, **Switch account** choosing another account, a reply saved in the older shape after reload, and a 390 px phone). The desktop mock can now fail a held reply with `failCapabilities`. No account was driven to a real limit, so the native shapes rest on the installed binaries and the saved replies. Logs and screenshots: `artifacts/usage-limits/`, `artifacts/usage-limit-desktop.png`, `artifacts/usage-limit-mobile.png`.

## Open sign-in waits only for its own console — 2026-09-30

The user reported that at some point every **Open sign-in** in Connections was disabled, with no error or explanation, so they could not sign in again. Their installed app is 0.6.0, released before 81cf452. In 0.6.0, Open sign-in is disabled while any reply runs on this computer (`localRunning`), which the chat asking about it already made true, and while any Connections action runs, and nothing says so. 81cf452 removed both conditions on `development`, and the installed app gets them with the next release.

The remaining conditions could still disable the buttons without a reason. `signingIn` was one value for the whole page, so a sign-in whose console took its time to open disabled every other account's sign-in. The console signals readiness within ten seconds, and a WSL account first resolves its CLI. Sign-in also required the account's own check to have answered with an installed CLI. At startup those checks wait for WSL discovery and every environment's CLI inventory. A check that failed outright (`detect_connection` rejecting, as when the connection registry cannot be read) recorded `installed: false`, which read **Install the CLI** and disabled sign-in until the next full refresh. Focus refreshes wait for every reply running here. Only one sign-in was followed afterwards (`pendingSignIn`), so opening a second one replaced the first.

`signingIn` now holds each opening sign-in by connection, or by provider for a provider's own login, and only that button shows Opening sign-in… and waits. `signInUnavailable` disables sign-in only when the account's check found no CLI (`installed: false` without `checkFailed`). The button's title then says to install the CLI and refresh Connections, and the row keeps its **Install the CLI** status with the CLI's message below. A check that has not answered leaves sign-in available, and so does a failed one. `checkConnection` marks a failure `checkFailed`, a session-only field of `ProviderStatus` that heartbeats never carry. The row then reads **Could not check this account** with the error beneath and a usage note to refresh Connections. The composer's warning for that account reads as unverified instead of **needs to be set up**. `pendingSignIns` follows every sign-in opened, each until its account reports ready or ten minutes pass, and the notice names the account (**Claude · Second Claude is connected. You're ready to chat.**). A CLI that updated itself (`applyCliUpdates`) rechecks its accounts in that environment, since a check made while the update replaced the executable may have found none.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,197 files), 560 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 316 browser scenarios (two shards of 158), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 353 Rust tests with 13 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/sign-in-availability/`. The desktop mock can now fail one account's check (`test-check-error-<id>`, thrown as a native error string), report its CLI missing (`test-cli-missing-<id>`), and hold sign-in consoles open (`holdSignIn`, released through `heldSignIns`), recording each sign-in in `signInCalls`. `tests/account-sign-in.spec.ts` gains three scenarios. First, after a reload whose checks are held, the account reads Checking… and its sign-in opens. A failed check reads Could not check this account and still opens sign-in. A missing CLI disables it with the explaining title, and a later check enables it again. Second, an active chat whose account check failed shows the unverified warning. Third, with one console held open, another account's sign-in still opens, and both accounts are followed until they report ready without a focus event. All three fail against the previous source. Existing scenarios now expect the notice naming the account. The failed, missing-CLI and opening states were reviewed in both themes and at 390 px with a throwaway spec. Not exercised: the native app, since the change is limited to the page and the mock drives the same commands, and a real CLI update overlapping an account check.

## The Agent picker shows each account's 5-hour and weekly usage — 2026-09-30

The user, who spreads their limits over several Claude and Codex accounts, asked to see each account's current 5-hour and weekly usage in the list of accounts, drawn as the bars below the chat. The Agent picker listed accounts with only their company or sign-in status, so the usage of any account but the selected one was visible only in Connections.

`ChoicePicker` takes an optional `optionAside` snippet, drawn between an option's name and its check mark, and a `width` for the list, which `anchorPopover` now receives with its trigger and updates while open. Options with an aside become a four-column grid, and below 460 px of list width (a container query on the listbox) the aside moves under the name. An empty aside takes no room (`:empty`). The option's `aria-describedby` points at the aside, so its text describes the option: **5-hour 70% used Ahead of pace Weekly 25% used Below pace**. The Agent picker asks for 560 px while any option shows bars, which leaves about 210 px for the account name beside two bars; the viewport bounds it.

`QuotaBars.svelte` draws an account's `visibleLimits` without a model, as the composer does: the label, the percentage used, the pace arrow and the 4 px bar with its fill and even-use guide, from the same global classes (`usage-meter compact-meter`, `quota-fill`, `recommended-fill`), so the colors, the over-guide segments and the striped unknown track match. The bars themselves are hidden from assistive technology. Their hover gives the reset time and pace, prefixed Last reported for an old or failed reading. Weekly takes the second column even when Codex reports no 5-hour window, so the columns line up down the list. Its clock is the shared one (`src/lib/clock.ts`), which runs only while the picker is open.

`listedUsage` in `+page.svelte` gives each listed account its latest snapshot (`snapshotFor` without a model, so a reading taken for the selected chat's model counts too). It shows Checking… from the moment an account can be read until its first reading arrives or fails, keeps a reading that can no longer be refreshed as last reported without pace, and shows nothing for an account marked Sign-in needed. The picker used to read only its Claude accounts on this computer, to renew their logins (see the entry below). It now reads every listed account that `canQueryConnection` allows, Codex, Gemini and remote accounts included, through the same transport and relay routes as Connections. It reads again when that set grows while it is open, as when an account's sign-in check finishes after opening. The native reader's one-minute cache keeps reopening cheap. A first version read only on opening; a scenario with the checks held at startup left an account on Checking… and fails against that version.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,197 files), 560 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 313 browser scenarios (two shards of 157 and 156), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 353 Rust tests with 13 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/agent-usage/`. `tests/agent-usage.spec.ts` covers four scenarios with the fixture's half-elapsed windows, wrapping the mock's usage reads to give an account percentages of its own or to hold a provider's reads. First, two Claude accounts, Codex and Gemini show their own values (96% and 64% for the second Claude account), amber where they are ahead of pace, with fill and guide painted over each other as below the composer and the description above. In a list over 500 px wide the bars sit right of the names with every Weekly group at one position, opening the picker read every account, and clicking an account's bars chooses it. Second, a held Codex reading shows Weekly Checking… on a striped track in its own column and then the values; after Codex is signed out it shows only Sign-in needed, with its aside hidden. Third, with sign-in checks held at startup, an account listed as Checking availability… gets its bars once the checks finish. Fourth, in a 390 px window the bars sit below the name and detail, within the list. Screenshots: `artifacts/agent-usage-picker.png` and `artifacts/agent-usage-picker-narrow.png`; the light theme was reviewed with a throwaway spec. Not exercised: the native app with real accounts (the bars use the same readings as Connections and the composer) and remote accounts through a real relay.

## Accounts that need signing in say so before a message fails — 2026-09-29

The user learned that an account was signed out only when a message failed on it: a chat switched to another Claude account answered **Failed to authenticate: OAuth session expired and could not be refreshed**. They then could not sign in, because every **Open sign-in** in Connections was disabled, with no reason given. They asked for the accounts list to show an account that needs signing in, and for choosing it to open Connections at that account.

The Agent picker gave every installed account its company as detail, whatever its reported sign-in. That status also goes stale, because Claude Code finds out that a login expired only when it next tries to renew it. A probe with Claude Code 2.1.284 and a throwaway profile holding an expired access token and an invalid refresh token showed the sequence. `claude auth status` first answered `loggedIn: true` in 264 ms, without the network. The app's usage query (`get_usage` over stream-json, `cli_queries::claude_usage`) then answered in 0.7 s with `rate_limits_available: true` and `rate_limits: null`, which the reader reports as **Claude did not return its usage limits**. Afterwards the CLI had blanked the profile's tokens, and `auth status` answered `loggedIn: false` (**Login: Expired — log in again**). The terminal's own login, expired since 2026-09-28, answers the same. The app rechecked sign-in at startup, on focus and after a sign-in, but focus refreshes wait for every reply running here, and with replies side by side that is most of the time. The disabled buttons were left over from when one reply ran at a time. Open sign-in was disabled while any reply ran on this computer (`localRunning`) and while any Connections action, such as Refresh connections, was busy. The poll that follows a sign-in also waited for every reply, although `sign_in` opens a console of its own and has nothing to do with replies.

The picker now marks an account whose status is installed and signed out, from this computer's check or the owning host's heartbeat, with **Sign-in needed** in `--warning` and a `CircleAlert`. `ChoicePicker` options take an `attention` flag for this, which also puts the detail into the option's accessible name, and the option's tooltip says what choosing it does. Choosing such an account selects it as before and opens Connections at it (`openConnections`). A first version only opened Connections, which the existing signed-out Gemini scenario showed to be worse: selecting still lets a draft be prepared with a signed-out account, and after signing in the chat is already on it. `FleetManager` takes the account as `highlight`: it scrolls to its card, marks it with the warning tint and a bar at its left edge until it is connected, and focuses its Open sign-in, or the card when the account belongs to another computer. The mark ends when Connections is left. A signed-out row on this computer reads **Sign-in needed** in orange instead of **Sign in or refresh**, which stays for an unverified login, and its Open sign-in becomes a bordered button. One on another computer whose host reports it signed out reads **Sign-in needed on its computer**. The composer's warning names the account (**Sign in to Claude · Second Claude before sending a message.**), and its Open Connections opens at that account.

`recheckConnections` checks named Claude and Codex accounts on this computer through `detect_connection`, whatever replies run, each at most every ten seconds unless forced. Opening the Agent picker rechecks the accounts it lists and reads the usage of its Claude ones, which renews each login; the native reader keeps a reading for a minute, so reopening stays cheap. Codex usage is not read there, since nothing showed that a revoked Codex login changes `codex login status`. A failed usage reading forces a recheck of its account after any check already under way, since that check may have read the login before the renewal failed. Open sign-in has its own state (`openSignIn`), so neither replies nor other Connections actions disable it, and WSL's Add account no longer waits for replies either. After a sign-in, `checkSignIn` checks that account alone every five seconds whatever replies run; a sign-in without an account connection still runs the whole refresh. A focus event while replies run checks a pending sign-in too. Refresh connections spins its icon while it runs, which explains the other controls it disables meanwhile.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,195 files), 560 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 309 browser scenarios (two shards of 155 and 154), and Rust formatting. Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Logs: `artifacts/account-sign-in/`. The desktop mock can now expire one account (`test-usage-expired-<id>`: its next usage read fails and its check reports it signed out, as Claude Code does), override one account's sign-in (`test-auth-connection-<id>`), and records the connection a sign-in opened. `tests/account-sign-in.spec.ts` covers three scenarios. First, opening the picker finds a second Claude account's lapsed login and lists it as **Claude · Second Claude, Sign-in needed** beside a normal one. Choosing it opens Connections scrolled to its marked row with Open sign-in focused, and the chat keeps its draft, takes the account and warns before sending. The composer's Open Connections marks the row again, and after sign-in the mark and warning go and a later visit to Connections marks nothing. Second, a usage reading on returning to the window finds the selected account's login lapsed, and the composer names it and opens Connections at it. Third, while a reply runs in another chat, every Open sign-in is enabled, and the sign-in completes and is noticed before that reply ends. Existing scenarios now expect the new wording, and the signed-out Gemini one passes through Connections when choosing the account. Screenshots: `artifacts/account-sign-in-picker.png` and `artifacts/account-sign-in-connections.png`; the light theme and a 390 px window were reviewed with a throwaway spec. Not exercised: the native app with a real lapsed login (the CLI side was probed directly, as above), an account on another computer marked through a real relay, and how Codex reports a revoked login.

## A sub-agent's conversation is kept whole — 2026-09-29

The user asked whether the 16-message limit on a sub-agent's conversation was fixed. It was not: 24 of the 31 sub-agents in their saved workspace had text cut at it. Their panels showed **Additional sub-agent text was omitted at the activity limit**, and the Blender sub-agent from the earlier reply kept only 16 messages from more than three hours of work. The decoder kept 16 messages per child, 4,000 UTF-16 units each and 16,000 in all. It also cut a child's result and summary at 8,000 characters and its task at 2,048. The saved-data schema enforced the same bounds on every device and on the relay, which validates each uploaded conversation with `sharedSchema`.

`AgentActivity::message` in `src-tauri/src/protocol/activity/subagents.rs` now keeps every message whole. A streamed one grows by its new text alone, instead of copying and re-measuring the whole message on each delta. Tasks, results and summaries are kept whole too (`WHOLE`), and the Rust `messages_truncated` flag is gone. `toolActivitySchema` drops the count, total and length bounds on a sub-agent's messages, task and result, and allows any safe `after`. Replies saved earlier keep their flag, and their panel now says **Some of this sub-agent’s text was not recorded: earlier versions kept only 16 of its messages.**

Every update of a sub-agent sends its group's whole record, and Codex streams a child's text a token at a time, so a longer record would cost more with each delta. Streamed Codex child text now goes out at most every 250 ms (`STREAMED_TEXT_EVERY`): `record` keeps a held update, and the group's next update, or the decoder's once-a-second tick, sends it. A completed message goes out at once. An open panel also rendered every message's Markdown again on each update. `renderMarkdown` now keeps the HTML of recently rendered text, least recently used first, within four million characters. Streamed intermediate versions are never asked for again, so they are the first to go. A throwaway measurement with 300 messages in an open panel and 60 updates put the main-thread time at 2,635 ms without the cache and 520 ms with it.

The relay and every app version check saved sub-agent records, so this reaches real use through a release. The relay and its Viewer deploy themselves within minutes of a release, before the desktop restarts into it and starts writing longer records. A device still on an earlier version refuses a longer record when it reads one, so its sync reports an error until it updates, and nothing is lost. The user's workspace has one computer, which runs every reply, and the phone Viewer.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,193 files), 557 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 303 browser scenarios (two shards of 152 and 151), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 348 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Artifacts: `artifacts/subagent-resume/`. New Rust coverage keeps 40 messages of 5,000 characters each, grows a streamed one without its control characters, and leaves a completed message unchanged. It also sends a Codex child's deltas once, holds the next two until the tick sends them together, sends a delta that is due, and sends a completion at once, whole. `src/lib/activity.test.ts` takes a sub-agent with 40 messages, a 20,000-character task and a 50,000-character result through the schema, a save and a sync. `tests/subagent-panel.spec.ts` opens a finished sub-agent with 40 messages, one of them about 10,000 characters, and finds all 40 without a truncation note. `tests/performance.spec.ts` streams 60 updates into an open 300-message sub-agent conversation, a budget of 25 ms per update on average: 14 ms with the render cache, and 45 ms without it, which fails. `docs/PERFORMANCE.md` records the rule and this scenario, along with the 3,000-call one added earlier today. Not exercised: a real Codex sub-agent streaming into the app.

## A resumed sub-agent stays one sub-agent, and its failed calls no longer read as its failure — 2026-09-29

After the 200-call limit went, the user asked whether the other problems found in the same reply still needed fixing. Three did. The collapsed sub-agent group read **1 background sub-agent** with **Failed** because two of the sub-agent's own 170 calls had failed: Claude Code refused a `sleep 60`, and a `tail` got bad options. The sub-agent recovered and finished both passes. Resuming it with SendMessage added a second sub-agent, and the first was renamed to a progress line (**Running Stop my Blender instance on port 9431**). Older replies still ended their activity with **Later calls not shown** and **Activity limit reached**, each marked **Outcome unconfirmed**.

A probe of Claude Code 2.1.284 with Haiku (`artifacts/subagent-resume/`) showed how a resume is reported. It launched a background agent, let it finish, then resumed it with SendMessage. The resumed task keeps its task id, which equals the agent id, but its `task_started` and final `task_notification` carry the SendMessage call's `tool_use_id`. The child keeps answering the Agent call as its `parent_tool_use_id`. The decoder took the reported `tool_use_id` first. In the user's reply the SendMessage call lay past the old limit and had no record, so the resume created a sub-agent under that id. With every call recorded, the resume instead landed on the **Message agent** call, which would have left the sub-agent reading as finished while it ran and given its outcome to the message call. The probe's resume kept the original description even after the child ran a command. The rename in the user's reply therefore came from an older CLI that started the resume under the Agent call, with the child's current step as its description.

In `src-tauri/src/protocol/activity.rs`, a task event now goes to the sub-agent whose call it reports. Otherwise it goes to the sub-agent the task already belongs to, and only then to the reported call or the task's earlier call. A start for a sub-agent that exists sets it running again. It names only a sub-agent still called by its placeholder, so a sub-agent its own call named keeps that name. `activityGroupSummary` counts a group's sub-agent calls apart as `childIssue`. The group's tense and outcome follow its own calls and sub-agents, and when those have no issue, the sub-agents' failed, blocked, stopped or unconfirmed calls show as a count, such as **Worked with 1 sub-agent** · **2 calls failed**. A sub-agent that failed itself still reads **1 sub-agent** · **Failed**. `activityEntries` turns an older reply's activity-limit record into the note **Calls after the first 200 were not recorded: earlier versions kept only 200 per reply.**, so the group before it is labelled by its own calls. The `limit` action kind and its presentation, which nothing reaches any more, are gone.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,193 files), 556 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 301 browser scenarios (two shards of 151 and 150; this includes the three rewind scenarios committed meanwhile in 1162a4d), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 347 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. New Rust coverage replays the probe's events three ways: resumed under a recorded SendMessage, under one the reply never recorded, and under the Agent call with a progress line as its description. Each time the one sub-agent keeps its name, runs again, then completes with the second summary, and the message call stays complete. With the previous routing, the first case left the sub-agent complete while it ran. `src/lib/activity-groups.test.ts` covers the count and its label, a sub-agent's own failure winning over its calls, and the limit record turning into a note after its group. `tests/subagent-panel.spec.ts` saves a reply whose sub-agent completed after two failed commands and that ends with a limit record. Its Work history reads **Worked with 1 sub-agent 2 calls failed**, the sub-agent row reads Completed, and the note follows, with no **Later calls not shown** or **Outcome unconfirmed**. On the previous frontend the same scenario read **1 sub-agent and later calls not shown Failed**. Not exercised: a real SendMessage resume inside the app, whose events the unit test replays as captured.

## Rewind here returns its message to the composer — 2026-09-29

The user asked for a rewind to keep the original sent message, and for Rewind here to stop asking which message to return to, since they had already clicked it. Asked where the message should go, they chose the message box, where Claude Code's and Codex's own rewinds put it, over keeping it in the chat with only the replies set aside. Before, the message left the chat for the rewind's saved suffix, reachable only through Undo rewind, so sending it again meant typing it again.

Rewind here now rewinds at once (`openRewind` calls `changeHistory`); the dialog and its message picker remain for /rewind, which has no clicked message. Either way the message returns to the composer ahead of any draft through `returnedDraft`, which reuses `restoreToDraft`, the path queued messages take after a stopped reply. Its text returns, and its images are read back into memory before the rewind is written (`rewoundMessage`, `draftImage` in `images.ts`). `readChatImage` now serves the desktop too, through a new async `read_chat_image` command around `chat_images::ensure` (this computer's store, or the relay). An image that cannot be read is left out, with a notice that Undo rewind restores the message with it. Codex mentions stay chosen only while the conversation uses the account whose reply answered the message; otherwise they become stale tokens to choose again, as after a scope change. Skills need nothing, since their slash command is part of the text.

`rewoundDrafts` keeps, for this window, what each rewind put in the composer and what it replaced, tied to the rewind's `createdAt`. Rewinding further back replaces a returned message left unchanged, so the composer holds only the newer message and the draft. Undo rewind takes a returned message back out while its text and images are unchanged, restoring the draft, and leaves an edited one. A rewind or undo that cannot be saved puts the chat and the composer back. The record ends with the next send or retry, a deletion or a workspace change, and after a reload Undo rewind leaves the composer alone. The rewound notice now says what the confirmation used to: files stay as they are, and the next reply starts a new agent session without earlier tool details. Its Undo rewind button no longer shrinks, since the longer notice squeezed it onto two lines on narrow widths; the other setup notices keep Open Connections on one line the same way.

Validation: `npm run verify` steps (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics in 5,193 files, 556 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 300 browser scenarios in two shards of 150 on the first run), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 346 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Screenshots and the native report: `artifacts/rewind/`. New coverage: `rewind.test.ts` reads a message back with its text, mentions and the answering account, a stored image and one saved inline, and counts an unreadable image; it merges the message ahead of a draft within the image limit, keeps mentions per account and scope, and recognizes an unchanged returned message. `tests/rewind.spec.ts` checks that a click rewinds without a dialog, returns the message ahead of the draft with the composer focused, starts no reply, and that Undo rewind takes it back. Rewinding further back replaces the returned message, an edited one survives Undo rewind, a reload keeps the returned message as the chat's draft, and a failed save leaves chat and composer as they were. /rewind still asks and returns the chosen message, at 390px too. A message's image comes back as an attachment read through `read_chat_image` and is sent again with the same hash under a new id. `tests/mentions.spec.ts` sends a native file mention again after a rewind. Natively, `scripts/rewind-images-native-smoke.mjs` ran against an isolated `rewind-qa` build (CDP 9593) with no provider. It kept a 64×48 PNG through `store_chat_image` and read its 272 bytes back as an `ArrayBuffer`, while a path-like hash was refused. It then rewound a seeded chat without an error banner, showed the image in the focused composer at its own width, and Undo rewind emptied the composer and saved the restored history. `scripts/rewind-native-smoke.mjs` follows the new flow but was not rerun, because it needs real Claude and Codex replies. Not exercised: a Viewer reading the images back from a real relay, and WebKit.

## Replies record every call, without the 200-call limit — 2026-09-29

The user saw **Later calls not shown** and **Activity limit reached** in a reply that ran for 6 h 40 min with a background Opus sub-agent, and asked why there was a 200-call limit and whether it could go. The limit came with the first commit (2026-09-09), when every save and sync carried the whole workspace. Tool results now stay on the executing computer (2026-09-25) and saves and syncs carry one conversation (2026-09-26), so a record is only metadata: across the user's 7,653 saved records the median is 489 bytes and the 99th percentile 3.4 KB. Seven replies from the last day had hit the limit. In the one the user saw, the sub-agent's 170 calls used most of it, so none of its second pass was recorded, nor were the main agent's later calls: messaging it, checking its files, the commit and the push.

`ToolDecoder::publish` in `src-tauri/src/protocol/activity.rs` now records every call, and `applyRunEvent` no longer stops at 201 blocks. Four internal limits would have broken long replies silently once the count was gone, so they are lifted too. Elapsed-time clocks were created only while a reply had fewer than 200 calls, and Codex progress bindings stopped at 200 items. The Claude task map, which attributes task notifications that carry no `tool_use_id`, stopped at 64 tasks. Codex sub-agent lifecycle and turn keys stopped at 256. Each holds one small entry per call, task or event for the length of the run. The schema-bound limits stay (64 sub-agents, 16 messages per sub-agent, 64 reasoning blocks), because an older Viewer or relay would reject larger records. Replies saved earlier keep their notice.

A remote run's relay job still sends every event it keeps with each update, and the released relay refuses more than 448. `retainRunEvent` now keeps at most 160 calls, since the other kinds of events are bounded at 271 together. A new call moves the oldest finished calls out, 64 at a time, instead of being dropped. The device following the job compares events by position, so after a move it applies the shifted events again, which every kind of event tolerates. A device that reads rarely gets the calls it missed with the conversation the executing computer saves and syncs, where `mergeActivityBlocks` matches them by identity. The relay needs no change. Until the next release reaches it, the Viewer it serves still stops adding calls at 201 while following a remote run, and the synced conversation brings the rest after the calls it holds.

Some paths were only fast because of the limit, and they are now linear. `mergeActivityBlocks` matched each incoming block by scanning the others: 2.5 s for 40,000 blocks, now about 10 ms. The timeline's `topLevel` scanned every call for each call. `activityGroupSummary` scanned every call for each group and copied every sub-agent call on every event; it now reads the distinct states of each sub-agent's calls, indexed once per list. The run callback scanned every block twice per event for a question, which only a question tool can start. `runningSubagents` filtered every call once per running sub-agent. In the Rust decoder, the task lookup no longer formats a string per recorded call, and the once-a-second elapsed-time update copies only the running calls instead of every call.

A new scenario in `tests/performance.spec.ts` streams a running reply's first 3,000 calls, every other one from a background sub-agent. It then streams 100 more while it measures, and opens the finished reply's Work history. A CPU profile of the measured phase in the development build showed 6.5 s in the 30 busiest functions, not counting the engine's own work, with the limit removed but before the linear fixes. That included 993 ms in `activityGroupSummary`, 349 ms in the question check and 245 ms in `runningSubagents`. After the fixes it showed about 3 s, with 110 ms and 130 ms left in the last two. One task went over 50 ms while streaming (53 ms), and none did while Work history opened; both budgets are 150 ms.

Validation: `npm run check` (0 diagnostics in 5,193 files), 553 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 297 browser scenarios (run in two shards of 149 and 148), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 346 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Profiles and timings: `artifacts/unlimited-activity/`. New Rust coverage records 1,000 calls and then one more, and 300 hooks and then one more. It also covers the lifted internal limits: a call after 300 others gets its clock and a Codex progress signal, the 100th background Claude task's notification finds its call, and a Codex sub-agent's 300th turn binds. `src/lib/activity.test.ts` runs 1,001 calls through `retainRunEvent`. It checks that no update exceeds 448 events or 160 calls and that a running call stays in the job. The executing computer's copy and a reader following every update both get every call in order, including a late update to a call that had left the job. A reader following rarely misses some, and the merge with the saved conversation restores them. The same file merges 40,000 blocks within 500 ms. `src/lib/activity-groups.test.ts` finds a sub-agent's failed call among 5,000 calls, including one appended after the previous summary. Against the previous `activity.ts`, the relay test recorded 201 of the 1,001 calls and the merge took 2.5 s. Not exercised: the native app with a real reply of thousands of calls, and a remote run from the Viewer beyond 160 calls, which the unit simulation covers.

## Sending from the end glides to the new message, and sending above it stays put — 2026-09-29

The user asked for a message sent at the bottom of a chat to scroll smoothly to the new reply instead of shifting the content all at once, and for a message sent while reading above the end not to scroll at all. `send()` used to set `nearBottom = true` whatever the reader was doing, and the follow paths jumped to the end in the frame the message rendered. A send now follows only from the end. This is measured again at send time, so a chat that fits its view, or a stale state left by a wheel over a short chat, still counts as the end. From there it arms a glide (`glide`) before the message renders. The paths that keep a followed chat at its end (`stickToEnd` in `scrollToEnd` and both resize observers) hold off while the glide runs. Each animation frame covers a share of the distance left, 1 − e^(−dt/80 ms) and at least a pixel, so output the reply writes meanwhile speeds the glide up rather than making it jump. It stops at the end or after 700 ms, and ordinary following carries on from there. The reader's wheel, pointer or keys in the conversation end it where it is. So does any scroll it did not make, such as find in page, which `readingPosition` recognizes against the position the glide last set. Following then resumes only from the end. Reduced motion keeps the instant jump, and a send from above the end leaves the reading position alone, with Jump to latest leading down.

Native smooth scrolling was measured first. Chromium runs it on the compositor, but it eases in over about 40 ms, and a second `scrollTo` mid-way restarts the curve from rest: when output arrived during the glide, the speed dropped from about 4 px/ms to almost nothing. In a production build at 240 Hz, the glide over 224 px took 68 frames (about 280 ms), with steps easing from 11 px to 1 px. Two of three runs had no frame interval over 5 ms, and the third missed one frame. The development build showed one 13 ms hitch as the reply started. A 25 fps recording showed the question and the start of the reply easing into view, without flashes.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 551 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 296 browser scenarios on the first run), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 345 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored (Rust is unchanged). Logs and video frames: `artifacts/send-glide/`. New coverage in `tests/chat-follow.spec.ts` records the scroll position on every frame. When a message is sent at the end, the chat takes more than five frames to get there, moving only downward, while the reply's text arrives mid-glide, and following continues afterwards. With reduced motion at most three frames show the message below. Sending 900 px above the end leaves the position unmoved, and the reply arrives below with Jump to latest marked live. A wheel, or a script scroll standing in for find in page, ends a glide mid-way, and the reply then arrives without moving the reader. Against the previous code, the first scenario recorded no frame with the message below, and the scenario sending from above the end moved the reader 1,124 px. Removing the reduced-motion check made its scenario record 65 gliding frames, and removing the wheel takeover or the check for scrolls from elsewhere let the glide run to the end. `chat-virtual-space.spec.ts` and `live-activity.spec.ts` each measured the end once right after a first message that now glides; they now wait for the glide to finish. A native `tauri dev` session and WebKit were not run.

## A Jump to latest button at the conversation's lower left — 2026-09-29

The user asked for a button that jumps down to the latest messages, placed on the left instead of in the middle, where chat apps usually put it. `+page.svelte` shows it (`jumpShown`) while the chat does not follow its end and the end, the top of the virtual space, lies more than 64px below the view: `readingPosition` updates it after scrolls and settled toggles, and both resize observers do too, because streamed output grows the conversation below a reading position without any scroll. Opening another chat clears it. Pressing it resumes following, clears held space and scrolls to the end at once; a smooth scroll would stop short of an end that streamed output keeps moving. The button disappears, so a key press moves focus to the composer. While the open chat's reply runs, locally or on another computer, its arrow and border take the accent.

It sits in the avatars' column, left of the prose, 8px above the bottom of the view, hanging from a zero-height `position: sticky` rail after the messages inside the message column. A first version sat in the composer area over the conversation, where a wheel or touch scroll starting on it scrolled nothing. That matters because the button appears while the reader scrolls up, possibly under a resting pointer or thumb. Inside the scroller, the scroll passes through it. On phones a 44px circle covered the first letters of the prose, so the button stays 32px with a 44px touch area from a pseudo-element. The rail's `z-index: 1` keeps it above messages and below the drop overlay, usage details and the top-layer menus and dialogs.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 551 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 291 browser scenarios on the first run), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 345 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored (Rust is unchanged). Logs: `artifacts/jump-to-latest/`. New coverage: `tests/chat-follow.spec.ts` on desktop and at 390px. It checks that there is no button while following, and that after scrolling up it appears marked as live, aligned with the reply avatar, 8px above the bottom of the view and clear of the prose, with the phone's larger touch area. The wheel over it scrolls the conversation while the button keeps its place, and output below leaves both the reading position and the button in place. Pressing it shows the end and follows later output. A 30px movement stops following without the button until output moves the end away, pressing Enter on it leaves focus in the composer, and once the reply ends the button is no longer marked. The existing follow, virtual space, scroll, composer growth, live activity, drawer swipe and sub-agent panel scenarios pass unchanged. Screenshots of the live, idle, hover and keyboard focus states were reviewed at 2x in dark and light, in Edge; a native `tauri dev` session and WebKit for the iOS Viewer were not run.

The user asked for the collapsed Model dropdown to drop the version from its mark, since the selected name beside it already shows the number, while the picker's choices and the reply avatars keep theirs, and for CLI default's mark to be as wide as the others. `ModelMark.svelte` no longer draws the version in its `chip` variant, so the trigger shows the selected line's 20px print before its name; a model of unknown line keeps the chip icon there, since without a version its mark would only repeat the provider. ## A chat opens at its end and keeps following it — 2026-09-29

The user reported that opening a chat often showed their latest message instead of the end, where the agent was still answering. Four paths left a chat there, and each reproduced in a new browser scenario before the fix:

- **Following carried over from the chat left behind.** `openConversation` scrolled to the end only when the reader had been at the end of the previous chat. After reading above the end of one chat, a running one opened at the same scroll offset, 2,848px above its end. Opening another chat now resets following, while choosing the chat already open keeps the reader's place. Move to history and Fork reset it themselves before and no longer need to.
- **Connections and Settings mount the chat again.** They replace the chat view, which came back at the top of the conversation with nothing scrolling it. Returning now shows the end of a followed chat, or else the message the reader left at the same distance from the top: an `$effect.pre` records the first message still in view by its new `data-message-id` before the view goes (`readingPlace`), since disclosures start collapsed again and a plain scroll offset would point elsewhere.
- **Updates received through the relay were never followed.** Only events of replies this window started called `scrollToEnd`. A reply another computer runs grew below the reader through `applyChats` (or `apply` on older relays), as does a Viewer's stale copy updated just after a chat opens, and a reply this computer runs for another device grew through `checkpointRun`; the chat stayed at the latest message, 1,790px above the end in the scenario. These now follow the open chat like streamed events (`followReceived`), including the held space that keeps a running reply in place while its calls fold.
- **Content that sizes itself after it renders.** A visual starts at 96px and grows when its frame reports its height, so a chat whose last reply held a 700px visual opened 681px above its end. A resize observer on the message column now keeps a followed chat's end in view when the column grows. Toggles keep their behavior: virtual space settles them in an animation frame, which runs before resize observers, so content a toggle reveals has already stopped following.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 551 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 289 browser scenarios on the first run), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 345 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored (Rust is unchanged). Logs: `artifacts/chat-follow/`. New coverage: `tests/chat-follow.spec.ts` opens a running chat after reading above the end of another (and keeps the place when the open chat is chosen again), returns from Settings and Connections to the end and then to a reading position above it, opens a chat whose last reply holds a visual, and follows a reply another computer publishes through a real relay; all four failed before the fix with the distances above. The existing virtual space, chat scroll, composer growth, live activity, Work history, sub-agent panel and visualization scenarios pass unchanged. The behavior is DOM-only and the browser checks use Edge, whose Chromium engine WebView2 shares; a native `tauri dev` session and WebKit for the iOS Viewer were not run.

## Sent files form galleries and open in one connected viewer — 2026-09-28

The user asked to navigate between send_files assets with arrows, connecting all of them, to group sequential images into a gallery, to pan and zoom opened images, and to show the 3D viewer larger in the chat.

- **Galleries.** Consecutive images of a group form rows of equal height that fill the reply's width, each tile the shape of its image (`SentFilesView.svelte`). `galleryRows` in `src/lib/sent-files.ts` picks the rows from the reported shapes alone, the split whose rows come closest to a target shape, so nothing is measured and nothing moves as the pictures load; a row of tall images stops at 320 pixels. `replyContent` joins single images sent one call at a time when their markers stand side by side (`loneImage`), each keeping its caption under its tile. In the user's history, groups held three to five images (1440 × 900 screenshots, 1672 × 941 concept art) that stacked one per row at up to 320 pixels each; five screenshots now take two rows of about 410 pixels together.
- **One viewer per chat.** `FileViewer.svelte` with the registry in `src/lib/file-viewer.svelte.ts` connects every image and model the open conversation shows, ordered by their place in the page, so the arrows, the arrow keys and a swipe on a whole image step across groups and replies. A model's full-size view is its `ModelView` snippet, which keeps its animation and turn while its inline view gives up its scene; a views-only model reads its views at once there. The dialog exists only while open: always mounted, it came first in the page and broke `tests/input-templates.spec.ts`, which reads `document.querySelector('dialog')`. A tool call's own images use the same viewer for that call alone.
- **Zoom and pan.** `ZoomableImage.svelte` places the image with one transform (math in `src/lib/image-zoom.ts`): the wheel zooms where the pointer is, a pinch follows the fingers, a double click or tap zooms in where it lands and back out, the buttons and the + - 0 1 keys zoom about the center, and dragging or the arrow keys pan a zoomed image.
- **Larger 3D.** The inline stage spans the reply's width at 16:10 (about 778 × 486 in a wide window, against 420 × 280 before).

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 551 unit/HTTP tests in 74 files, production build, 285 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 345 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/file-viewer/verify.log`, `rust.log` and `rust-tests.log`. New coverage: `image-zoom.test.ts` (fit and tiny images, zooming about the pointer and its limits, panning bounds after a resize, double-click toggling, wheel and pinch steps), `sent-files.test.ts` (runs across groups, aspect bounds, balanced rows for two to eight screenshots and mixed shapes, joining only single images side by side) and `tests/file-viewer.spec.ts` (gallery rows sharing heights and the width, stepping from a gallery into the next reply's model and back with arrows and keys, the inline model giving up its scene, focus returning to the tile, wheel zoom keeping the pointed point, drag and key panning without stepping, double click, Fit, 1:1 and 0, a swipe stepping, joined single images with their captions). Existing send_files, model viewer, turntable and tool result scenarios passed unchanged.

Real data: two of the user's chats (21 images and a textured character model across three replies, and 11 app screenshots across four) were loaded with the files their computer keeps into the desktop mock and rendered in headless Edge, WebView2's engine: the galleries, the full-width model, the viewer stepping through all 22 files of the first chat and zooming a 1672 × 941 image were reviewed from screenshots, which stayed in `artifacts/file-viewer/`. Not exercised: the native desktop app, a real phone's pinch (the pinch shares the tested pointer path) and the Viewer over the relay.

## Sub-agents open their conversation beside the chat — 2026-09-28

The user asked for sub-agents to stop expanding inside Work history: their running conversation should open in a sidebar on the right, and the bottom of the reply should show the sub-agents that are running, the way it shows running background tasks.

- **Side panel.** `SubagentPanel.svelte` opens one sub-agent of a reply beside the chat: the task as the first message, its messages among its own calls through the chat's `ToolActivity` (live rows while it runs, groups and call details with results from the executing computer), the sub-agents it started, and its result once it finishes. It docks at the right on wide windows with its own remembered width (`PanelResize.svelte`, generalized from the artifact divider, whose root class is now `panel-resizing`), is a drawer otherwise, and takes turns with the artifact side panel. It closes with its conversation, on leaving chat, or when its reply no longer holds the sub-agent.
- **Rows and the footer toggle.** Work history and live activity list sub-agents as rows (`subagentRows`) instead of expanding their messages, calls and result inline; a running reply's footer has a **Sub-agents** toggle (`RunningSubagents.svelte`) listing those still running with what each does now, from its latest call. Sub-agents launched in the background moved there from Background work, which now counts commands and monitors only, so none is listed twice.
- **Order.** Child messages carry `after`, the number of the child's calls the decoder had recorded when the message began, so the panel places text among calls (`subagentTimeline` in `src/lib/subagents.ts`). Saved messages without it follow the calls.
- **A name that stayed put.** The decoder renamed a Claude sub-agent after every `task_progress` event, whose description says what the child does now ("Reading beta.txt"), so the panel header and footer list would have shown its latest step as its name. Only `task_started` names it now.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 543 unit/HTTP tests in 73 files, production build, 282 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 345 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/subagent-panel/verify.log` and `rust-tests.log`. New coverage: Rust tests for Claude blocks keeping their place before the calls of their own line and for Codex messages counting only the child's calls, and a `task_progress` that no longer renames its child; unit tests for finding sub-agents, running ones with their latest call, nested records and their status, placing messages (past the recorded calls, without a place, empty ones), results replacing only the closing messages they repeat, the panel's status and the schema's `after`; browser scenarios on desktop and phone for the footer list, the panel following a running sub-agent into its result, a nested sub-agent opening in place, docking beside the chat with Escape and the drawer on phones, Work history rows opening saved conversations after a reload, long tasks and commands shortening instead of widening rows (which failed before the fix), and the updated background work, sub-agent detail and capabilities scenarios.

Real provider: Claude Code 2.1.283 (Haiku, this conversation's profile, the chat's stream flags including `--forward-subagent-text`) delegated reading two files in a throwaway folder. It launched the sub-agent in the background (`is_backgrounded`, `isAsync`) and streamed its three text blocks between its two Reads. The actual decoder, run over the captured stream in a temporary probe, placed the messages at 0, 1 and 2 and kept the name "Read two text files and extract capitalized words", which the probe first showed being replaced by "Reading beta.txt". Its ten published events, replayed through the browser mock, showed the panel live (**Running in the background**, 2 calls) and finished, with the closing report shown once as the result (`artifacts/subagent-panel/real-*.png`). The terminal Claude login (`~/.claude`) still reports an expired OAuth session. Not exercised: a native desktop run, real Codex sub-agents (decoder fixtures only), and WSL. Until a release deploys the relay, it drops `after` from synced history, so messages there follow the calls.

## 3D models stay at full detail, and other devices see eight views — 2026-09-28

The user asked for models to stop being compressed before they are shown and suggested that, if sync was the reason, other devices could see a fallback image or renders from eight directions. The reason was the relay: a model was capped at 12 MiB so one relay request carried it whole, and the send_files guidance told agents to reduce the mesh to fit. In the user's history an agent exported a Draco-compressed preview of a tree to get under the cap, and another described a 24.5 MB character as needing decimation first.

- **No shrinking.** A reply shows models up to 1 GiB (`MODEL_BYTES`), what one window loads whole, and the guidance tells agents to send a model at full detail, never decimated, with smaller textures or compressed for the viewer. The Draco, meshopt and Basis refusal now says to export uncompressed at full detail. glTF and GLB documents are checked as they stream into `GltfDocument`, which keeps only where buffers and images come from and the extension lists, so a document with hundreds of megabytes inline is never held whole. Sizes past a gigabyte read as GB.
- **This computer.** Its window reads a model as raw bytes through `read_tool_output_model_file` (a `tauri::ipc::Response`), without the base64 and JSON a relay request needs. A model larger than the window's 64 MB cache is read only when its scene is built, instead of once ahead and again for the scene.
- **Other devices.** The base64 read (`read_tool_output_model`) stays within 12 MiB (`RELAY_MODEL_BYTES`) and says to show views past it. Another computer's larger model appears as a turntable of eight views (`modelShownAsViews`), requested through the new `toolOutputModelViews` relay job. The owning host's window answers with the views kept beside the model, or draws them with `renderModelViews`: the viewer's lights, starting camera and framing, 45° per view in the direction a drag to the right turns the viewer, the first animation's opening pose, and a mid grey for colourless models, as square 1600-pixel WebP pictures. It draws one model at a time, simultaneous requests share one drawing, and `store_tool_output_model_views` keeps them as `model-<n>-view-<k>.webp` with `model-<n>-views.json` written last. Only eight PNG, JPEG or WebP images of at most 4 MiB each and 12 MiB together are kept, beside a kept model; a missing view leaves none listed, and a new `modelViewsRenderer` draws them again. The turntable turns by dragging, the arrow keys, Home and End, and Turn left and Turn right, inline and expanded, and reads as a slider (View N of 8). The model never crosses the relay.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 537 unit/HTTP tests in 72 files, production build, 279 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 344 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/model-views/verify.log` and `rust-tests.log`. New coverage: Rust tests for documents checked as they stream (a 3 MB inline buffer before an image beside the file, a URI that is not text, a cut document) and gigabyte sizes, a model past one relay request read raw on its computer and refused to the relay with the views notice, and the views store (seven views, a GIF, undecodable data, a model that was not kept, views past the total, the round trip with renderer and dimensions, a missing view, views going with a result recorded again). Unit tests cover the views schemas, the relay's validation of the new job and its result leaving once read, and the host drawing once for a relay job and this window's simultaneous request, keeping and reusing the views without reading the model again, redrawing an older renderer's views, and `modelShownAsViews` at the 12 MiB boundary. Browser scenarios cover a 300 MB model opening whole on its own computer through the raw read without reading ahead; `renderModelViews` drawing a one-sided triangle from the front in views 1 to 3 and 8 and not at all in 4 to 7, which fixes the start and the turning direction; and, in `tests/federation.spec.ts`, two desktops paired through a real relay, where the MacBook's window draws the views of a 40 MB model for the Desktop, which turns them by key, button and drag and expands them while the MacBook reads its model once and keeps the views.

Native: `scripts/model-views-native-smoke.mjs` drove an isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.model-views-qa` build (`scripts/native-model-views.tauri.json`, CDP 19801, cold scratch target 1m17s) with a local relay serving the Viewer. A real Codex reply (CLI default, GPT-6-Astra) sent a generated 208 MB torus knot of 8.4 million triangles through send_files. The app opened it whole in 3D: the raw read took 1.2 to 1.4 s, and the scene drew 1.7 s after the chat was opened following a restart. The base64 read that serves the relay refused the model with the views notice. The first views request through the relay took 3.1 to 3.7 s while the host drew eight 1600 × 1600 WebP views of 60 to 90 KB, a repeated request took 0.3 s from the kept views, and the Viewer showed the turntable, turned it and expanded it with no page errors (`artifacts/model-views-qa/`). The first 1800 × 1200 views left the model small in the nearly square inline stage, so views are square. The Claude run failed first because the terminal Claude login (`~/.claude`) reported "OAuth session expired and could not be refreshed"; it needs signing in again, which this check did not touch. Views drawn in the browser from two models in the user's history (the textured Explorer character and a tree) came out at about 25 KB each and matched the viewer. Until a release deploys the relay, the production relay refuses the new job method, so other devices cannot get views of a model over 12 MiB through it.

## Parked CLI processes stay until their chat is closed — 2026-09-28

A parked Claude or Codex process was released after fifteen idle minutes, and parking a fifth chat evicted the oldest, which also ended any Monitor it still ran. The user asked for neither to happen: the user decides when to close a chat, and the chat's process closes with it. `src-tauri/src/pool.rs` no longer has `IDLE_LIMIT` or `MAX_PARKED`. The pool keeps one process per conversation for as long as its chat waits, and its minute sweep only drops processes that exited on their own, reporting the background work that ended with them. Closing a chat means moving it to History (the toolbar or a sidebar row) or deleting it. On the computer that runs the chat, `closeConversation` in `transport.ts` calls `release_conversation`; the Viewer or another desktop sends a new `release` relay job (`{ conversationId, connectionId }` only, validated by the relay) to that computer instead. A chat deleted on another device is also released when the deletion reaches its computer through either sync path (`apply` or `applyChats` in `+page.svelte`). History moves arriving by sync release nothing: each desktop's startup moves its saved chats to History and publishes that, and it must not close another computer's chats. Rewinds, file Undo, a changed launch and Quit still end the process, and closing to the tray keeps it. A package-manager Codex update now waits until those chats are closed or the app quits.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 534 unit/HTTP tests in 72 files, production build, 276 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 341 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/parked-processes/`. New coverage: `pool.rs` tests park six conversations without evicting any, and keep an idle process through a simulated day while sweeping one that exited. `relay.test.ts` accepts a `release` job only with exactly a conversation and a connection. `tests/parked-processes.spec.ts` checks that Move to history from a row and from the toolbar, and Delete, each release that chat's process, and that a new start of the app releases none. With a real relay, a chat deleted on another device is released here and one moved to History there is not. A paired Viewer's Move to history sends a `release` job to the owning computer. No native run: the pool change is covered by its tests, and `release_conversation` is unchanged. The production relay accepts the `release` job only after the next release; until then a Viewer's Move to history leaves the process running until the chat is deleted or the app quits.

## Chats whose monitors still run stay marked in the sidebar — 2026-09-28

The user asked for an icon on a chat whose reply has ended and been read but whose Monitor is still active, so the sidebar shows that the chat is still waiting for the monitor. Once no reply runs, a chat whose host still lists a Monitor watch for it (`hostBackground`, the list the reply footer's Background work toggle reads) ends its row with the `Activity` pulse that Work history gives Monitor calls, in `--accent-strong`, until each monitor reports its outcome or the process is released. The mark doesn't depend on reading, so an unread chat shows it beside its green dot. The row's hover title and accessible name add Monitor still running, or the number of monitors (`rowNotes` in `+page.svelte`, which also keeps the existing New reply note). A running reply's time and a waiting question take precedence, and background commands never mark a row, since the servers and watchers left for the user usually run until they are stopped. The host list exists only on the executing computer, so the Viewer and other computers show no mark, as they show no Background work after a reply.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 533 unit/HTTP tests in 72 files, production build, 273 browser scenarios) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. A reply that ends while another chat is open shows the dot and the pulse, with `· New reply · Monitor still running` and `, new reply, monitor still running`. Opening the chat keeps the pulse, which sits left of Move to history on hover. Two monitors and a server read `2 monitors still running`, a server alone or an empty list removes the mark and the extra accessible name. Zoomed dark, light, hover and phone-drawer captures with a running, a monitoring and an unread monitoring chat side by side (`artifacts/sidebar-monitor/`) were reviewed.
