## Closing the window keeps Agent Studio in the system tray — 2026-09-28

The user asked for closing to move the app to the tray, as the Claude and Codex desktop apps do, instead of quitting it.

- **Closing hides.** A close request (the title bar, Alt+F4, the taskbar's Close window) hides the window while the tray icon is shown, so replies, queued sends, relay jobs from other devices, parked CLIs, update checks and alerts carry on. The hidden page keeps running unthrottled, since WebView2 still reports it visible, but loses focus, so alerts for the chat it shows are no longer suppressed and its relay foreground lease ends. **Quit Agent Studio** in the tray menu takes the previous close path: it stops work on this computer, releases parked CLIs and installs a downloaded update when idle. Hiding does neither.
- **The tray.** `src-tauri/src/tray.rs` adds the icon through Tauri's `tray-icon` feature, with **Open Agent Studio** and **Quit Agent Studio**. A left click opens the window on Windows; macOS opens the menu, and Linux reports no clicks. The menu handler is registered once at startup, because Tauri never removes menu handlers and an icon added again would otherwise quit once per earlier icon, and the icon is built and removed on the main thread. On Windows the icon loads the application icon's hand-tuned small drawings at the tray's size instead of scaling the 32-pixel window icon. Linux looks for an AppIndicator library before building the icon, because tray-icon aborts the app without one; the packages already depend on it.
- **One copy.** `tauri-plugin-single-instance` 2.4.5 (2.5 requires Tauri 2.12) is the first plugin, so a second launch shows the running window and exits before reading saved data. Without it, reopening Agent Studio from the Start menu while it sat in the tray would have started a second process on the same workspace. macOS shows the window again from the Dock (`RunEvent::Reopen`).
- **Settings → Background.** A switch, on by default and saved per device in `tray.json`; turning it off removes the icon, and closing quits again. A computer without a tray shows the reason with the switch disabled. The notification text now says alerts need the app running, including in the background.
- **QA tooling.** `npm run start:windows -- -SecondInstance` launches a running build again and reports whether it handed over. `scripts/tray-native-helper.ps1` sends the icon's own callback message, as Explorer does, so the pointer never moves, and chooses menu items with the menu's keyboard handling, since UI Automation does not see a menu whose app is not in the foreground. Native drivers that end the app from the title bar call `quitOnClose()` from `scripts/native-page.mjs` once they connect.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 533 unit/HTTP tests in 72 files, production build, 271 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 341 Rust tests with 12 opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/tray/verify.log` and `rust-tests.log`. New coverage: `tray.rs` tests (the default and a saved choice, hiding only while the icon exists and before Quit, the status shape), `window-behavior.test.ts` (wording on each platform, an unavailable tray, the schema), `tests/background.spec.ts` (the switch, a reload, a failed save, an unavailable tray, macOS wording) and the Viewer check in `tests/app-updates.spec.ts`.

Native: `scripts/tray-native-smoke.mjs --reply` drove the isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.tray-qa` build (`scripts/native-tray.tauri.json`, CDP 19751, cold scratch target 1m26s). A real Claude Haiku reply, closed to the tray as soon as it started, finished complete while the window stayed hidden, and its alert reached Windows' notification history; a tray click then showed the reply. Closing hid the window with the process running and the page unfocused; a left click on the icon and **Open Agent Studio** each showed it again; the menu held Open Agent Studio, a separator and Quit Agent Studio; a second launch exited with code 0 and brought the hidden window to the foreground, leaving one process; the Settings switch removed the icon and wrote `{"closeToTray":false}`, then restored both; **Quit Agent Studio** ended the app within two seconds; with the switch off, a title-bar close quit, and the choice held across restarts. Reports: `artifacts/tray/native-report-reply.json` and, for the final build, `native-report.json`; screenshot: `artifacts/tray/native-settings.png`. Not exercised: clicking a toast while the window is hidden (the tray's own `show_window` path), macOS and Linux (their icons, the Dock and the AppIndicator check), a relay-started reply while hidden, and quitting from the tray during a reply, which runs the same code as the previous close.

## Images live apart from the chats that show them — 2026-09-27

The user chose to take image bytes out of the chat data, the last proposal of the review. Each attached image was base64 inside its message, so every save, sync patch, checkpoint and conversation download of that chat carried it, a phone received every image of every chat it synced, and a conversation past a 48 MB patch could not sync at all.

- **References.** A message now keeps `{id, name, mediaType, hash, bytes}` per image, the hash being the SHA-256 of the bytes, so a desktop, a browser and the relay that each turn one inline image into a reference produce the same record and merging finds nothing to reconcile. The bytes live once per computer in app data `images/<hash>` (`src-tauri/src/chat_images.rs`), which windows show through the async `studio-image` protocol, and once per workspace in the relay's `images/<hash>` (`relay/images.ts`). The relay takes an upload only when its bytes are a PNG, JPEG or WebP of at most 16 MB matching the hash it was sent under, streamed to a temporary file, and checks access again before naming it.
- **Sending.** The composer keeps images before the message names them: the desktop through `store_chat_image` with the raw bytes as the IPC body, the Viewer by uploading to the relay. A failure keeps the draft and its images with the reason, and a draft edited while its images were kept goes out when it is sent again.
- **Sync.** Before a conversation, or a reply for another computer, names an image, the relay must hold it: a desktop uploads the images it holds that the relay lacks, asking about each hash once per connection, and a conversation naming one nobody here holds waits on this device with a notice. The whole-state path held nothing: its upload went out whole or not at all, so one such conversation during a connection's first sync would have kept every other one from syncing. It now sends the rest, keeps that conversation as the relay last had it, and hands it to the incremental polls after it. Against a relay from before the store (no `images: 1` in its revision answer), image conversations stay on the device.
- **Replies.** The executing host reads each image a provider receives from its store or the relay, checks its hash and keeps it; the bytes exist in the request in memory only. Native session fingerprints serialize references, and a binding saved while images were inline continues once they become references.
- **Older copies.** Desktops move images saved inline into the store when they load the workspace and the sync checkpoint, and relays when they load a workspace, each keeping `workspace-before-images.json`. The relay still takes inline images from older apps, stores them, and answers those apps (no `X-Studio-Images: 1`) with the bytes inline again in state, conversation and job answers.
- **Exports and pruning.** Exports put the bytes back inline so they stand on their own, and count the images neither this computer nor the relay holds. A desktop removes images its saved workspace no longer names an hour after they were kept, at startup and after each reply; the relay removes those no conversation, rewind or waiting run names a day after upload, at most hourly.
- **Attaching.** The new `readImage` decoded with `createImageBitmap`, which refused a PNG with a wrong checksum that an image element shows, as the templates scenario found. It decodes with an image element again, from an object URL instead of a base64 data URL.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 529 unit/HTTP tests in 71 files, production build, 269 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 338 Rust tests with twelve opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/image-store-verify.log`. Unit and HTTP tests cover the relay's image routes, compatibility answers, startup move, pruning (a fresh upload kept, and a rewind's and a waiting run's image kept until the run is forgotten), the streamed size limit, an invalid inline run image and access revoked mid-upload, and the transport's publishing, holding on both paths, handoff to incremental polls, remote-run checks and old-relay behavior; 31 mutations of the relay and transport code were each caught. Rust tests cover the store, the migration and export round trip, pruning, and a pre-store session binding. Browser scenarios cover a sent image kept by hash and absent from the saved workspace, a draft edited during the store (confirmed to fail without the guard), and two app environments running a remote reply whose image the relay holds. `scripts/images-native-smoke.mjs` now checks the stored reference and the protocol address of the reopened preview; it and a real provider reply with an image were not run in this pass. The link-mark scenario now polls for the opened page, which a busy run once checked before the opener's first import finished.

Follow-up the same day: the plan also covered the Viewer's saved copies, which that commit left holding images inline. The Viewer's copy and its checkpoint are the only saved data nothing converted, so a Viewer kept that bulk in IndexedDB until each conversation changed, and a conversation edited there before the relay was updated compared an inline baseline with the relay's references. On connecting to a relay with the store, the Viewer now hashes each inline image once, uploads those the relay lacks from the copy itself, and turns both its copy and its checkpoint into the references the relay made of the same images, rewriting the checkpoint with its revisions. An image the relay refuses stays inline. The sync notice for a conversation too large to send no longer says images live inside conversations; the whole-state message drops the claim that other conversations still sync, which its whole upload never did. Validation: a `browser-transport.test.ts` case with one image the relay already holds, one only the Viewer has and one the relay refuses, and seven mutations of the conversion, each caught; the full pipeline was run again before pushing.

## Question alerts carry their question, and interrupted replies still earn their dot — 2026-09-27

From the same review, the notification findings.

- **The first AskUserQuestion alert never showed its question.** Claude reports the tool as it begins, seconds before the question is recorded, and the tool alone already produced the `attention` key, so the desktop and the relay both announced "Your agent asked for your input." and then dropped the question as a duplicate. The desktop tracker now waits up to five seconds (`questionGrace`) for the question, asking the window for another look when the wait ends, and sends the generic line only if none arrives. On the relay, an alert still held takes the newest line for what it announces, on both the checkpoint and the job path.
- **Replies running when a window started never got a dot or an alert.** The saved copy of such a reply reads interrupted, and the trackers took that as its end, so the real end synced later was not news. A reply saved as interrupted now has not finished until a reply says so. A reply that started this session also counts however long it ran (it needed to start within five minutes of being first seen), and both trackers keep their runs in recency order within twice the chat count, where more than 2048 chats used to rotate opened chats back to marked.
- **The relay.** An alert held for a chat deleted meanwhile is dropped; each chat remembers its last eight readers, so a session revoked later no longer hides an earlier reading; alert text is built only for an alert that goes out or is refreshed; the saved queue's limits follow the text limits and an unreadable saved alert is dropped instead of refusing the whole file; and comparing checkpoints looks conversations up by id instead of scanning for each one.
- **Text.** Every HTML tag is removed, character references are decoded, a comment in a code span stays text while one outside code hides the lines until it closes, indented code and link or footnote definitions are dropped, and long answers and errors are read only as far as the line needs (a 5 MB error took about half a second). Direction controls and invisible marks are removed in the app and again natively, and a line is never cut inside a flag, a family emoji or an accented letter. MCP input alerts no longer name the server, whose name comes from the CLI configuration, and a phone alert that replaces an unread one of the same reply still rings.

Validation: unit tests for the question grace and its wake, a question recorded two seconds after its tool, interrupted replies finished elsewhere, a twenty-minute reply first seen finished, three thousand chats observed repeatedly, the refreshed held alert on both relay paths, a deleted chat, a revoked second reader, an unreadable saved alert, the Markdown cases, direction controls and grapheme cuts; eight of them were confirmed to fail against the reverted behavior. `tests/desktop-notifications.spec.ts` now checks that a question tool followed by its question gives one alert carrying the question, and a Rust test covers the native removal of direction controls. Read records stay in memory only, so a relay restart during a hold still loses readings made during it.
## The model viewer keeps a bounded number of scenes and rebuilds what it gives up — 2026-09-27

From the same review, the viewer findings.

- **Scenes piled up past the browser's limit.** Each model built a WebGL context the first time it scrolled into view and kept it until the reply unmounted, so a chat with enough models passed the roughly sixteen contexts a page may hold and the browser blanked the oldest without a word. `src/lib/model-viewer.ts` now keeps at most eight scenes per window: a scene out of view for ten seconds, or the one out of view longest when another model needs its place, gives its context back and is rebuilt with its camera and animation time when it returns, on a canvas of its own since a released context cannot draw again. A context the browser takes back is rebuilt the same way, twice before the stage says so. One stage builds one scene at a time, so leaving and re-entering during a slow parse no longer leaves an orphan drawing forever.
- **Reads, decoding and first frames.** A model was read as soon as its reply opened and its base64 held in the component; it is now read when its stage comes within 600 px of the chat's scroller, which is the observer's root because the window cannot see past that scroller however wide the margin, and the cache holds decoded bytes. Decoding goes through `Uint8Array.fromBase64` or a `data:` fetch instead of a loop over every character, the loader follows the format the host recognized, shaders compile through `compileAsync`, a scene is built only after its stage stays on screen for 150 ms, an animation alone draws at most 60 frames a second, and nothing autoplays under reduced motion.
- **Pause, replacement and Retry.** Pause stopped the action, which snapped a rigged model to its rest pose and restarted it at zero; it now pauses the action, and leaving the screen stops drawing without touching the animation. A group sent again under its id kept the old model on screen; each stage is keyed by the model it names. Retry after a drawing failure left an empty stage; it now rebuilds, and glTF 1.0, FBX older than 7 and a device without WebGL 2 are named.
- **Textures, URLs and cleanup.** FBX textures kept beside the file (or never embedded) left materials multiplying by black; the viewer waits up to five seconds for textures a loader starts in the background and drops those that never arrived. Refused URLs became `''`, which a fetch reads as the page itself; they are now an empty `data:` URL that fails locally. Textures, image bitmaps and the object URLs FBXLoader never revokes are released. A model without colours of its own takes the theme's new `--model-surface`, which reads on the light background too, and the camera now fits the model to the narrower of the two directions.
- **Chat and keyboard.** The inline canvas gave the wheel and every touch to the controls, so a phone could not scroll the chat over a model; it now removes the controls' wheel listener and asks only for horizontal swipes. The canvas is focusable with arrow-key turning, +/- zoom and Home to reset, the expanded view has zoom buttons, and the inline figure keeps its place while the expanded view is open, so focus returns to Expand on close. Structured-output replies now show their files and visuals after the JSON.

Validation: `tests/model-viewer.spec.ts` adds seven scenarios, each confirmed to fail against the reverted behavior (reading ahead without the scroller root, no scene limit, no key, no touch rule, a pause that stops the action, structured replies without files): a model far above the end read only when scrolled to, twelve models never holding more than eight scenes and one rebuilt on return, a replaced group showing its new model, a WebGL failure named and redrawn by Retry, touch and wheel handling with focus returning to Expand, a pause holding the animation time on and off screen, and a structured reply showing its image after its JSON. `model-viewer.test.ts` covers handing places out and waking a waiting stage, and `model-scene.test.ts` now uses three.js r186's own messages. The existing viewer, texture and structured-output scenarios pass unchanged. The parsed model is still parsed again for the expanded view, and the Viewer still precaches the three.js chunk with its shell.
## Files a reply shows are checked and kept as the reply numbers them, and large reads cross the relay — 2026-09-27

From the same review, the send_files and relay findings.

- **A file that could not be kept shifted the ones after it.** The reply numbers each image and model by its position in the call, but copying skipped a file that vanished or grew between the check and the copy, so the next file answered to its number and the last one to none. Kept files now keep their place: a missing one leaves an empty entry that reads as not kept, and the copy stops at the size the file was checked against.
- **A GLB was judged by its first 512 KB.** A document longer than that went unchecked, so a buffer beside the file or a Draco extension listed after a long node list reached the viewer as an empty box. The GLB header is now read (a glTF 1.0 container and a cut file are named), the whole document chunk is parsed, and Draco is refused whenever it is listed, since three.js prepares that decoder for any document naming it; `KHR_meshopt_compression` joins the refused extensions, and a byte-order mark before a glTF document is read past as the viewer does.
- **One call could hold a reply, and its Stop, for minutes.** Each path was checked without a deadline, so a share or mount that never answered kept the reply waiting for eight of them. One call's checks now finish within 30 seconds together.
- **Paths and captions.** Device paths (named pipes, `\\?\GLOBALROOT`) are recognized by their parsed prefix instead of two string prefixes; a WSL file name with a backslash, a colon or a trailing dot or space, which Windows reads as another file, is refused; caption tabs and other control characters become spaces as line breaks already did; and the tool schema says the caption appears below the files, where the reply shows it. Files a reply shows also count as its answer when it writes no text.
- **Large results could not cross the relay.** A kept 16 MiB image is about 22 MB as base64, past the 20 MB the relay accepted for a job update, and the 40 MB it held for all of a workspace's requests fitted one such result, kept for ten minutes. The relay now accepts updates up to 24 MB, holds 64 MB, and drops a finished read once its requester has it (a minute after it finished otherwise); a window reads two large results at a time from another computer; model reads get the same deadline as images (two minutes now); and the browser's 30-second limit applies to stalls, with an upload given time for its size, instead of to a whole transfer. A result the relay refuses now ends its read with that reason instead of expiring as a lost computer.
- **A reply started from another device reported questions late.** Its events were sent by a 700 ms repeating timer, which a hidden window runs about once a minute. Questions, MCP input requests and question tools are now sent at once, and other events 700 ms after they arrive from a timer the event starts; updates made while one is waiting share it.

Validation: Rust tests for Draco listed but optional, required meshopt, a glTF 1.0 GLB, a cut GLB, a document past the sniffed head with a buffer beside it, a byte-order mark, device paths, the bounded copy, stable image and model numbers, caption control characters and WSL names; relay tests for the update limit, the storage budget and result retention; and transport tests, each checked against the reverted change, for a refused result and for a question sent without waiting for a timer.
## A Viewer tab can no longer lock the Viewer out of its saved copy — 2026-09-27

From the same review. Each Viewer save writes its index and the conversations it names, and removes the entry of any conversation missing from its own index. A second tab that had not received a conversation yet therefore removed that conversation's entry while the first tab's index still listed it. Reading refused an index with a missing entry, which left the browser on the storage error until its site data was cleared. Reading also took the index and the entries in two transactions, so a write between them could produce the same refusal.

`readBrowserWorkspace` now reads the index, the checkpoint and every conversation entry in one transaction through the store's new `snapshot`. A listed conversation whose entry is gone opens as the sync checkpoint holds it, so the relay's copy merges over it on the next sync. One the checkpoint lacks is left out, and returns from the relay if it ever reached it. Keeping two tabs fully consistent would need one writer at a time, which remains a separate change.

Validation: `browser-workspace.test.ts` replaces the refusal case with one where a stale tab removed two entries: the synced conversation opens as the checkpoint held it, and the unsynced one is left out.

## Sync publishes streamed replies, stops whole passes and bounds its transfers — 2026-09-27

The user asked for a review of the send-files, notification and sync work of 2026-09-25 to 09-27, then for the findings to be fixed. This entry covers the sync and saving ones. Each was reproduced first: in a scratch copy of HEAD with the performance harness, and with vitest cases that mirror the page's own apply.

- **Replies stopped reaching other devices as they streamed.** The incremental sync publishes only conversations named through `takeUnsynced`, and answer text, tool calls and progress bumped `localChanges` without naming their conversation. Only reasoning (at an interval of fifty save durations), plans, questions and the reply's end did. Four seconds of answer text reached the relay zero times across four polls. Every run event now marks its conversation for the next sync through `src/lib/change-marks.ts`, while saving to disk keeps its own rate. A reply routed to another computer is published by that computer, so a window only saves its copy of it.
- **Everyday actions still saved and merged everything.** Starting a reply, changing the model or reasoning, a new chat, Move to history, steering, forking, deleting, rewinding, Undo, templates and Claude instructions all used the bare `persist()` or `saveSoon()`. Each was a whole-workspace save, and the next poll merged every conversation in one synchronous task: after a send on the 2.4 MB fixture, 24 of 24 conversations in 171 ms, with a 183 ms frame gap. Each call site now names what it changed: `persistChat` and `saveSoon(id)` a conversation, `persistSettings` and `saveSettingsSoon` the replicated settings, `persistLocal` and `saveLocalSoon` this device's own choices. Settings are marked apart from the conversations, so a settings change, or one received from another device, no longer makes a poll visit every conversation. After the change a send merges one conversation in 19 ms, with no gap over 50 ms. Received data is saved without being marked for sync, where it used to be republished on the next poll.
- **A removed Undo rewind came back on every device.** `apply` and `applyChats` used `Object.assign`, which never deletes a field. A device that received a conversation after Undo rewind, or after the next send, kept its old `rewind` and published it back. Every device then offered Undo rewind again, and pressing it appended long-removed messages after newer ones. `replaceFields` drops the fields the incoming copy no longer has, keeping the object.
- **A reply that ended during a patch upload flipped back to running**, on this device and on the relay. The incremental path applied a merge computed before `await …/patch` over whatever changed here meanwhile; the whole-state path had always merged again. The apply now merges again over any conversation edited since the merge read it, with that read as the base, and publishes the result next poll. Local reads are memoized per poll, which also cut the per-poll merge of the fixture's longest chat from about 38 ms to 17 ms.
- **The first whole-state sync of a connection dropped changes made while it ran.** It called `takeUnsynced()` after its network calls, discarding marks it had never read. It now takes the marks its read covers and hands them back when it fails, and every incremental poll that publishes nothing does the same.
- **Watching another device's reply rewrote the whole checkpoint every poll.** The throttle only counted local runs: during six remote updates an idle desktop wrote all 24 conversations five times. The checkpoint is now written at most once a minute, from any cause. The page first saves whatever the checkpoint holds, because after a crash an older saved copy would win the merge against the relay. A checkpoint that fell behind catches up on the next quiet poll.
- **Idle polls downloaded the manifest**, about 45 bytes per conversation every 2.5 s. They now ask for the relay's revision first and read the manifest only when it moved. A checkpoint's revisions are trusted only when they name exactly its conversations. The whole-state path used to write an empty list when the relay reported none, and trusting that would take every conversation for one the relay no longer has. A vitest case restarted with such a checkpoint lost both conversations without the guard.
- **Connecting downloaded the whole state** just to read the relay's identity, and the desktop client refused answers over 21 MB and any request over 15 s. That limit dated from the 20 MB cap 85c9518 removed, so a workspace past 21 MB could not connect at all. Connecting now reads `GET /v1/state/revision`, whose answer names the workspace. A desktop still downloads the state from a relay whose answer lacks the workspace name, since a missing name means the owner's. The Rust client has a connect timeout, a send limit that grows with the body, a 30-second idle limit per chunk and a 256 MB answer cap, instead of the total timeout, which reqwest arms at send time.
- **The relay accepted unbounded uploads** on a service capped at 512 MiB, and buffered each about four times over. A whole or patched workspace upload is now limited to 64 MB, refused from its declared length before it is read, and answered with 413 `too_large`. Devices send patches of at most 48 MB. A conversation too large for one stays unpublished with a notice in the sync status while every other one syncs, and the relay keeps its own copy, so nothing written here is lost. `POST /v1/state/chats` answers within 32 MB and names the rest, and devices read them in turns. The interim limits hold until image bytes move out of the chat data, the project the user chose next.
- **Phone-started replies saved on every event.** `checkpointRun` awaited a save per event, and each save rewrote and fsynced the whole file in Rust. Such replies now save at once only what `savesAtOnce` names (plans, questions, file changes, sent files and the like) and their end, like a reply started here.
- **Publishing at once was skipped when a poll was under way.** A question now publishes at once like a reply's end, and both wait for a poll already under way (`pollRelayNow`) instead of being dropped by it.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 483 unit/HTTP tests in 70 files, production build, 261 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 331 Rust tests with twelve opt-in cases ignored. New coverage:
- `change-marks.test.ts`.
- `transport.test.ts` cases for:

## Codex stays current too — 2026-09-27

The user asked for Codex to update the way Claude Code now does. Codex has the same gap. Its own update check lives in the interactive terminal (`~/.codex/version.json` was last checked on 5 September), Agent Studio runs `codex app-server`, and this computer kept 0.153.4 while 0.157.1 was out.

`codex update` exists, but unlike `claude update` it reruns whatever installed Codex every time it runs (`codex-rs/tui/src/update_action.rs`). That is the standalone installer (`powershell -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -c "$env:CODEX_NON_INTERACTIVE=1; irm https://chatgpt.com/codex/install.ps1 | iex"`, or `curl … install.sh` on Unix), a global `npm`/`bun`/`vp`/`pnpm` install, or `brew upgrade --cask codex`. The standalone installer:

- verifies SHA-256 digests;
- installs each release into `~/.codex/packages/standalone/releases/<version>-<target>` and switches `current`;
- deletes only `.staging.*` folders;
- answers its own prompts "no" when non-interactive;
- skips the download when the latest release is already complete.

So `cli_updates.rs` compares `codex --version` (now parsed from `codex-cli 0.153.4`) with the latest `@openai/codex` on the npm registry, and runs `codex update` only for a newer release or when the registry cannot be read.

A package manager replaces a Windows installation's files in place, which fails while a process runs them. Such an installation therefore waits, in a new Waiting state looked at every half hour, until no reply runs here and the pool (`holds_native`) keeps no Codex process. A standalone installation, recognized by its canonical path under `packages/standalone/releases/`, never waits, and neither does Linux. Codex's own messages add a Managed outcome ("Could not detect the Codex installation method…"), and a failure reason replaces Codex's echo of the whole installer command with "The installer failed with status …".

Settings becomes **CLI updates** with a switch per CLI. `cli-updates.json` now holds `{claude, codex}`, and the development-only `{automatic}` reads as both on. Statuses carry their provider, Connections shows the Codex version, and an update refetches that CLI's model catalogs.

Validation: `npm run verify` found zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, passed 464 unit/HTTP tests in 69 files, built for production and passed all 258 browser scenarios on the first run. Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 330 Rust tests passed, with twelve opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/cli-updates/codex-*`. New coverage:

- version parsing for both CLIs;
- `newer`, including a pre-release installed ahead of the latest;
- `codex_plan`: current, update, update without a readable latest, and waiting with and without a known version;
- standalone detection from a real `packages/standalone/releases` tree versus an npm script;
- Codex's outcomes: updated with its success line, unknown install method, installer failure;
- the Waiting schedule, the per-CLI switches and the pool's `holds_native`;
- stand-in Claude and Codex CLIs updating through the real process path;
- `tests/model-marks.spec.ts` now renders both switches, a Codex installation waiting with its message, and both Connections version rows.

Native, with the user's approval: an isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.codex-updates-qa` build (CDP 19781) listed six Codex models with Codex 0.153.4. Its automatic check reported Claude Code 2.1.283 up to date and updated Codex from 0.153.4 to 0.157.1, 72 seconds after launch. `current` now points at `releases/0.157.1-x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`, with 0.153.4 kept beside it. Three seconds later the Model list included GPT-6-Sol and GPT-6-Luna, which 0.157.1 adds. Settings read "Updated from 0.153.4 to 0.157.1 at 15:37." and Connections "Version 0.157.1 Updated from 0.153.4". A console-window watcher saw only the QA launcher's own terminal at launch, and none during the update.

Not exercised: an npm, bun, pnpm or Homebrew Codex (including the waiting path on a real installation), and a WSL distribution's Codex.

## Claude models are named for what the installed CLI runs — 2026-09-27

The Model picker offered Claude's aliases as Opus (latest), Sonnet (latest) and so on, but Agent Studio passes the alias itself (`--model opus`) and the CLI decides what it means. The user found their Opus replies running Opus 5 while Opus 5.5 existed. The CLI the app launches, `~/.local/bin/claude.exe`, was Claude Code 2.1.278, installed on 21 September; its binary does not contain `claude-opus-5-5` at all, and it resolves `opus` to `claude-opus-5`, while the 2.1.281 bundled with Claude desktop resolves it to `claude-opus-5-5`. Every one of the 93 Opus replies with a reported model in the user's workspace reported `claude-opus-5`, so "(latest)" had promised a model this CLI never ran.

`models::catalog` now asks the selected profile's CLI which model each alias means, the way a chat selects one: after `initialize`, a `set_model` control request per alias and then a `get_context_usage` summary, whose `model` is the resolved ID. One process answers all four in under a second here, without a prompt: no tools, `--safe-mode --strict-mcp-config`, in the usage runtime folder rather than the app's working directory (the Windows launcher starts the app in its checkout). Safe mode still applies a profile's settings: a throwaway profile whose settings map `ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_OPUS_MODEL` to `claude-opus-4-8` resolved `opus` to it with and without safe mode, and aliases resolve without a sign-in. `claude_name` turns an ID into the name Claude gives it (Opus 5.5; Haiku 4.5 without its snapshot date; no `[1m]` or `us.anthropic.` prefix). An alias the CLI refuses or leaves unanswered keeps its bare family name, an ID of any other shape (a gateway's own model) keeps its spelling, and the frontend's fallback catalog no longer says "(latest)" either. Reply headings already preferred a reported model over the alias but showed it as `Claude-Opus-5`; `formatModelName` now reads Claude IDs the way the catalog does.

A parked chat process would still have hidden an update of the CLI. Its launch fingerprint named the executable's path only, so a conversation that keeps replying reused a process started from the old binary for as long as it stayed busy. The fingerprint now also holds the size and modification time of the CLI file a native launch runs (npm's script, or the binary), so the first reply after an update starts the new version and resumes the native session, as any changed launch setting does. A managed WSL install's Linux file is not checked.

## The phone drawer follows a finger from the left border — 2026-09-26

The conversation drawer could only be opened and closed through its 44px buttons, which a thumb reaches badly. `src/lib/drawerSwipe.ts` now tracks a touch: closed, one that lands within 28px of the left border, and open, one that lands anywhere the drawer or its backdrop covers. The travel decides who keeps it — under 10px nothing is claimed, a mostly vertical drag goes back to the list or conversation it started on, and so does a drag away from the direction the drawer can move. Once claimed, the touch is the drawer’s: `preventDefault` keeps the page below from scrolling and suppresses the click a swipe across a conversation row would otherwise fire, the long-press menu having already cancelled itself at 8px.

The page keeps the progress in `drawerDrag`, which the shell exposes as `--drawer-progress` beside a `drawer-dragging` class, so the drawer moves with `translateX(calc((var(--drawer-progress) - 1) * 100%))` and the backdrop dims with the same number; the backdrop is now mounted for the whole phone layout, hidden and `inert` while the drawer is closed, rather than appearing with it. Releasing settles on the distance covered, or on the speed of the last 100ms when it is a flick — measured against the release, so holding the drawer still before lifting leaves the distance to decide. The state then changes exactly as the buttons change it, including focus moving into the drawer or back to the menu.

Only a released swipe animates, through a `drawer-settling` class the page holds for 400ms: a transition on `.sidebar` itself also ran when a desktop window was narrowed past 650px, which left the closed drawer visible for its duration — `getByRole('dialog')` then matched both it and a real dialog, breaking the phone scenarios in `rewind.spec.ts` and `input-templates.spec.ts`. The buttons stay instant, as before. Ordinary scrolling keeps the compositor’s fast path: the non-passive `touchmove` listener exists only between an accepted `touchstart` and the end of that gesture, a modal dialog, confirmation or open menu takes no swipe at all, and neither does a start inside content that can still scroll that way itself.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 421 unit/HTTP tests in 63 files, production build, 250 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 304 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored (Rust is unchanged). Log: `artifacts/drawer-swipe/verify.log`. New coverage: `drawerSwipe.test.ts` covers the progress of both directions, the direction lock and what each state claims, settling on distance, on a flick either way, and on a drag that stopped first; `tests/mobile-layout.spec.ts` drives real touch events through CDP at 390px — a vertical drag and a drag that starts away from the border leave the drawer closed, a slow 54px drag hands it back, a 244px drag holds it 76px off screen under the finger with the backdrop dimming, and the swipe back across the open drawer takes it away again, with focus, the backdrop’s reachability and the settling transition checked at each end. Removing the settle rule and rebuilding made that last assertion report `snapped`, so it is not vacuous. Screenshot: `artifacts/drawer-swipe/drawer-following-finger.png`. Not exercised: a physical phone — the gesture is driven by emulated Chromium touches, so iOS Safari’s own edge-swipe navigation is untested, and the native app, which is unchanged frontend code in the same engine.

## Image attachments lost their conversation budget — 2026-09-26

The user asked for the image limits to go now that saving and syncing move one conversation at a time. Three existed: sixteen — then four — per message, 2 MiB per image, and 8 MiB per conversation. Only the last was there for the workspace: it is gone from `images.ts`, from the page's attach handler, and from the native `RunRequest::validate`, and a conversation now holds as many images as it likes. `checkImageBudget` is deleted with it.

The other two are not about storage, so they were raised rather than removed. One message becomes one provider request and one decoded preview each, and `imageSchema` guards data arriving from a synced workspace, so an unbounded field would let a damaged or hostile copy make a device allocate without limit. Sixteen images of up to 16 MB covers a batch of 4K screenshots — a 4K PNG of a UI runs 1–6 MB, where the old 2 MiB was the actual friction. Nothing encodes what a provider accepts: Agent Studio pipes images to the Claude and Codex CLIs rather than calling an API, the two do not share a limit, and a provider that accepts less now reports its own error instead of the app pre-empting it with a wrong number. The remaining texts read from the constants, so raising them again is one edit.

Raising the per-image limit exposed a bug the old one had been hiding: `imageSchema` checked base64 with `(?:[A-Za-z0-9+/]{4})*`, a group repeated once per four characters, which recurses in V8's regex engine and threw `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded` on a 16 MB image — about 22 MB of base64. The alphabet is now one character class and the groups of four are checked by length, which is linear and rejects the same strings: a stray `=`, three padding characters, and any length not a multiple of four.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 417 unit/HTTP tests in 62 files, production build, 249 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 304 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/image-limits-verify.log`. Coverage: `images.test.ts` replaces its replayed-bytes case with one that accepts a full-size image and a full message of them and rejects one image past the per-message count — the first of those is what caught the regex overflow; the Rust `rejects_unsupported_roles_providers_background_requests_and_malformed_images` now expects five messages each carrying a full-size image to validate. Not exercised: a real provider with a large attachment, so the CLIs' own limits are unmeasured; the app reports whatever they answer.

## The Viewer and the checkpoint stopped copying everything — 2026-09-26

The two remaining per-reply whole-workspace paths, from the question the entries below left open.

The Viewer kept its copy under one IndexedDB key, so a phone following a streamed reply serialized its whole workspace each time a checkpoint arrived — the desktop patch never applied to it. It now keeps one entry per conversation beside a `:index` entry holding everything else and the id order, written through `saveBrowserWorkspace`. A save names the conversations that changed exactly as the desktop one does, a conversation that is gone has its entry removed in the same transaction, and `BrowserWorkspaceStore.update` took the write guard `put` already had so a save queued by a session that has ended still writes nothing. Reading assembles the index and its entries, and refuses a workspace whose index lists an entry the store no longer has rather than opening one with a conversation missing. A copy an earlier release wrote whole under the scope key itself is still read, and the next save replaces it and removes it; the localStorage migration also checks for the index, so it cannot mistake the new layout for an empty store. `transport.ts › saveWorkspace` is gone from `allowedWorkspaceCopies`: the Viewer no longer copies the whole workspace anywhere.

The sync checkpoint served the baseline for the next start of the app, and `saveCheckpoint` serialized that baseline once only to compare it with the last written copy. The baseline is always the relay's own state at one revision, so the revision identifies it exactly and the comparison is now free. The checkpoint also carries the revisions it came from — the relay revision, the per-conversation revisions and the meta revision — so a restarted app polls incrementally straight away instead of syncing whole once to learn them. A checkpoint written before those fields existed resumes the old way. Both sync paths now write through the one helper, before advancing the baseline in memory, so a failed write is retried.

The performance guard covered `workspace` only, which is how `JSON.stringify(baseline)` slipped past it; it now flags snapshots, clones and serializations of `baseline` as well, verified by reintroducing that call and watching `saveCheckpoint` appear in the failure.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 417 unit/HTTP tests in 62 files, production build, 249 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 304 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/viewer-checkpoint-verify.log`. New coverage: four `browser-workspace.test.ts` cases cover writing one conversation per save and reading the workspace back whole, removing a deleted conversation and refusing to write past a changed session, reading and replacing a copy an earlier release wrote whole, and refusing an index whose entry is missing; `transport.test.ts` adds a case where a connection resumes from its checkpoint and its first poll asks only for the manifest. `tests/viewer-storage.spec.ts` now checks the migrated cache is in the current layout with one entry per conversation and no whole copy left. Not exercised: a physical phone, and the production relay, which still needs a release before any device uses the incremental routes.

## Conversations sync one at a time — 2026-09-26

The second half of the structural fix. The relay's disk format is version 2: beside the workspace it keeps the revision that last changed each conversation and the revision that last changed the rest of it, upgrading a version 1 file by dating everything it holds at its current revision. Four routes use them — `GET /v1/state/manifest`, `GET /v1/state/meta`, `POST /v1/state/chats` and `POST /v1/state/patch` — and the whole-state routes stay exactly as they were, now recording what a whole upload actually moved so a device on either path sees the same history. A patch whose revision is stale is refused with the current manifest rather than a workspace, and data a device already holds never looks new to the others: an upsert equal to the stored conversation bumps no revision.

A poll compares the manifest with the revisions it agreed on, which gives the conversations that moved there, and `takeUnsynced` gives the ones that moved here. `mergeShared` reads whole workspaces and takes an absent conversation for a deletion, so `src/lib/incremental-sync.ts` restricts all three sides to the same involved ids: those either side changed, plus any present in the baseline but missing from one side, which is the only way a deletion is visible. Everything else is left alone. The plan it produces sends what the relay lacks, applies what this device lacks, and splits the deletions, since only the relay's own conversations can be removed there and only this device's here. A stale patch keeps its conversations unpublished and retries on the next poll.

One correctness trap found by the template sync scenario: a workspace with no conversations and a new template had nothing involved and no moved meta, so the poll returned early and never published it. Only a save that names no conversation can change the replicated settings — and that is also what marks every conversation — so such a poll now compares them even with nothing else to do.

Measured on a copy of the real 17.1 MB workspace, fifteen seconds of streaming with a paired relay. Before: 51.2 MB uploaded and 51.2 MB downloaded, 2,837 frames. After: 6.7 MB uploaded, nothing downloaded, 3,469 frames, and no long task in either run. The probe's bridge understates the old cost, because the app serializes each whole upload with one `invoke` on the page's own thread. Restarting still costs one whole sync: the checkpoint holds the baseline but not the relay's revisions, so the first poll takes the whole-state path and learns them from its answer. The remaining large write is that checkpoint, which a streaming reply rewrites at most every fifteen seconds.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 412 unit/HTTP tests in 62 files, production build, 249 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 304 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/incremental-sync-verify.log`. New coverage: `incremental-sync.test.ts` covers which conversations a poll looks at for every kind of change on either side, merging a subset without mistaking the untouched ones for deletions, the send/apply/remove/forget split, settings sent only when they differ, and the baseline keeping its order while dropping and appending; `transport.test.ts` adds three cases against a relay that answers the incremental routes — publishing one conversation while never downloading the other, taking in one that changed elsewhere, settings and deletions on both sides, and a patch refused because another device wrote first, still published by the next poll. Its existing whole-state cases now run against a relay that answers the manifest with 404, which its fixture proves is asked exactly once. `tests/performance.spec.ts` requires every upload of a streaming reply to carry one conversation, and its idle and waiting scenarios now expect manifest reads with no conversation moving in either direction. All 249 browser scenarios run against the real relay, so the two-device, conflict-copy and workspace-isolation scenarios exercise the new protocol. Not exercised: the production relay, which needs a release before any device uses these routes, and the native app.

## Saves send one conversation instead of the whole workspace — 2026-09-26

First half of the structural fix the entry below named. A save still writes one `workspace.json`, and every reader, export, backup, native smoke script and the Windows startup guard is untouched; what changed is who assembles it. `persist()` and `saveSoon()` now take the id of the conversation that changed, `saveWorkspace` passes those conversations with an index (the workspace without its conversations) and the full id order, and `save_workspace_patch` completes the file from the workspace its last write left in `StorageState.saved`. The order list carries additions, removals and reordering, so a patch is complete rather than a delta; a conversation that is neither sent nor already saved returns the whole-workspace notice and the page retries with everything, and the Undo command, which writes the file itself, clears that cache. A bare `persist()` means every conversation, so the seven converted call sites are the only partial ones: streamed run events, a relay-started reply's checkpoint, a background-work tool update, the reply's end, and generated or fallback titles.

Measured on a copy of the real 17.1 MB workspace, 15 seconds of streaming with no relay so only saving is timed. Before: 2 saves, 34.1 MB serialized through IPC, 2 long tasks blocking 209 ms, longest 114 ms. After: 14 saves, 15.8 MB, and no long task at all, at the display's full 239 frames per second. The reply saves seven times more often than before and costs nothing while doing it, because the adaptive interval from the entry below measures a cheap save and returns to its one-second floor.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 403 unit/HTTP tests in 61 files, production build, 249 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 304 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/chat-persistence-verify.log`. New coverage: four Rust `workspace_patch_tests` cover filling unsent conversations from the last write, additions and removals and reordering through the order list, the whole-workspace notice for a conversation that is neither sent nor saved and for one sent but left out of the order, and a malformed index; `tests/performance.spec.ts` now requires every save of a streaming reply to carry one conversation. The desktop mock in `tests/desktop-helper.ts` applies patches the same way, so all 249 browser scenarios exercise the new path wherever a reply saves. Not exercised: the native app, and a pre-migration workspace saved by a patch, which cannot happen because the first save after a migration sends everything.

## Streaming replies stopped saving the whole workspace every second — 2026-09-26

The entry below left stutter while a reply streamed, and attributed it to the chat re-rendering each event. A CPU profile says otherwise: the top self time was `structuredClone` and Svelte's `clone`, together 2.4 of 15 seconds, and the mock counted 19 whole-workspace saves in that window. `persist()` took `$state.snapshot(workspace)` and handed the copy to a save that serializes it again, and streamed reasoning asked for a save every second. Rendering barely appeared at all: 38 ms of `getBoundingClientRect` and no layout in the top twenty.

Two changes. `persist()` now passes the live workspace, since Tauri's `invoke` serializes its arguments with `JSON.stringify` and the browser store stringifies too, so the copy only walked every conversation a second time; reading through the state proxy costs about half of a snapshot followed by the same serialization. The queued save captures the workspace object rather than the variable, so it writes that workspace's newest state and a later reassignment cannot redirect it. And no rate makes a whole-workspace save free — at 16.6 MB it blocks tens of milliseconds, which drops frames on a 239 Hz display every time it runs — so streamed reasoning now saves at fifty times the last save's measured duration, between one and fifteen seconds, and `saveSoon` collapses requests made while a save runs into a single save after it. Plans, questions, file changes, visualizations, steering, compaction and the reply's own end still save immediately, and the relay still publishes progress every poll from memory, so other devices follow a reply exactly as before; only streamed reasoning can lag on disk, and only until the reply ends.

Measured on a copy of the real 16.6 MB workspace, 15 seconds of streaming (about 330 reported events), before and after. With no relay, which isolates saving: 13 long tasks blocking 2,310 ms with 18 saves became 3 blocking 278 ms with 6, and the longest task 188 ms became 102 ms. Relay-paired: 13 tasks blocking 2,952 ms became 4 blocking 413 ms, the longest 335 ms became 114 ms, and painted frames rose from 2,596 to 3,068 in 15 seconds. The probe's frame-stall totals are inflated when the relay is paired, because its `exposeFunction` bridge ships each 16 MB upload through CDP where the app uses one `invoke`; the long-task numbers and the profile are not affected.

This is not a Tauri or WebView2 effect. The probe runs the app in headless Edge against a mocked `__TAURI_INTERNALS__`, so every freeze found on 2026-09-24 and 2026-09-26 reproduces with no native side at all. They are all the same shape: work proportional to the whole 16 MB workspace, on the thread that also paints. What remains during streaming is the relay publish, about 170 ms per 2.5 s poll at this size, spent validating, merging and comparing the whole workspace; the structural fix for that and for saving is per-conversation persistence and an incremental sync, which needs a relay protocol change.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 403 unit/HTTP tests in 61 files, production build, 249 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 300 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/stream-save-verify.log`. New coverage: `tests/performance.spec.ts` streams 120 reported events, 40 of them reasoning, and requires fewer than twelve whole-workspace saves, at least one, and locally no task over 150 ms. Reintroducing the snapshot in `persist` fails `performance-guards.test.ts`, whose `persist` allowance is now gone. Not exercised: the native app, since the change is page code, and a second computer following a streaming reply over the production relay.

## The saved workspace has no size limit — 2026-09-26

The user asked for the 20 MB workspace cap to be removed; theirs had reached 16 MB, and a save past the cap failed outright. Every check of a workspace total is gone: the native `write_workspace`, the relay's `save`, `forkFitsWorkspace` before a fork, and `fitsWorkspace` before a send with images. Two readers of the saved file took only its first 20,000,001 bytes and then called it oversized, which would have started dropping conversations instead of failing; both now read it whole through one `saved::bytes`, next to `saved::fleet`, which already did. The relay kept the cap twice: its own write check, and the 20 MB request limit that `PUT /v1/state` shared with every other route, which would have become the new cap. That route now has no limit, since the relay holds the whole workspace in memory for as long as it runs anyway; other routes keep the 20 MB guard, and the two 8 MB tool-output streams still fit one request.

Removing `fitsWorkspace` also removed two whole-workspace copies: sending a message with an image copied and serialized the entire workspace, and so did every fork. Its `heldQueues` set existed only to hold a background queue that no longer fit, and is gone; a stopped or failed reply and mentions from another account still hold a queue through their own checks. Both `allowedWorkspaceCopies` entries for those functions are removed, so the guard now fails if either copy returns.

What replaces the cap is the honest constraint: the workspace is one JSON document, saved whole after every change and uploaded and downloaded whole when it syncs, so its size sets the cost of every save and sync rather than a point of failure. The docs say that instead of naming a number, and the rule keeping tool results out of the workspace keeps its real reason.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 403 unit/HTTP tests in 61 files, production build, 248 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 300 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/workspace-limit-verify.log`. New coverage: `transport.test.ts` syncs and saves a workspace past 25 MB through a real merge, PUT and checkpoint and then settles; the Rust `reads_a_workspace_past_the_former_twenty_megabyte_limit` writes a 25 MB workspace file and reads its registry and conversations back whole. The removed fork-limit test and its export are gone. Not exercised: a real workspace past 20 MB through the production relay, which needs a release first, and the Viewer's IndexedDB quota, which surfaces storage failures as it already did.

## Polls no longer freeze a chat while a reply runs — 2026-09-26

The user reported a small freeze every few seconds. Watching `%LOCALAPPDATA%com.vinicius.agentstudio` showed `sync-state.json`, 7.3 MB then, rewritten every 2.5 seconds, and a probe that loaded a copy of the real workspace (11.6 MB by then) through the desktop mock and a real relay found the cause: `settled` excluded any chat with a reply in flight, so its polls always took the slow path, whether or not the reply had produced anything. Each one copied the workspace with `$state.snapshot`, validated it, downloaded and validated the relay's copy, merged, uploaded, validated the answer and rewrote the whole checkpoint. None of it showed as a long task, because every relay request breaks the work into chunks under 50 ms, so the probe measured `requestAnimationFrame` gaps instead: frames stopped for 300–500 ms at the poll's cadence.

A reply already counts each of its events as a local change (`localChanges++` on every event and `persist()` at the end), so the revision alone decides whether a poll has anything to publish and the run conditions are gone from `settled`. A poll that does publish now asks `GET /v1/state/revision` first, and when the relay still holds the baseline it merges against the copy in memory instead of downloading and validating it again. After a `PUT`, the relay's answer is this poll's own data, so it is reused unless its serialization differs, which is how an older relay that drops a field is still noticed. `runtime.shared()` validates the live workspace directly: the validation detaches it anyway, so the snapshot before it was pure cost — 90 ms against 215 ms for that workspace. While a reply runs, the whole checkpoint is written at most every fifteen seconds; only the next start of the app reads it, and an older baseline merges conservatively.

Measured with the same 11.6 MB workspace, twelve seconds per phase, before and after: a reply waiting on a tool call went from 4,267 ms of stalled frames (worst 514 ms) and five full downloads to nothing at all, and a streaming reply from 3,423 ms (worst 566 ms) and 35 MB of checkpoint writes to 3,547 ms (worst 415 ms) and none. The remaining stalls there are the chat re-rendering each streamed event, not the poll, and are a separate cost. Idle chats were already free and stayed so.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 403 unit/HTTP tests in 61 files, production build, 248 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 299 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Log: `artifacts/poll-freeze-verify.log`. New coverage: `tests/performance.spec.ts` adds "a reply waiting on a tool call neither downloads nor rewrites the whole workspace", which runs on CI too and fails on the previous code; `transport.test.ts` replaces its running-reply case with one that publishes each change, downloads nothing, reads nothing while the reply waits, and writes the checkpoint again once the interval passes. `performance-guards.test.ts` lost both `pollRelay` workspace-copy allowances. Not exercised: the native app and a second computer following a reply over the production relay; the change is page code with its own tests, and the relay protocol is untouched.

## Site marks in the Viewer — 2026-09-26

The user asked why the phone needs the relay to mark links. It does because the relay serves the Viewer and the policy it runs under (`img-src 'self' data: blob:; connect-src 'self'`), so that window can neither load a site's icon nor fetch its bytes; the relay reads them instead. `relay/icons.ts` repeats the host's reader: `/favicon.ico` first, then up to three icons the page declares, bounded at 256 KB for an icon and 256 KB of page, accepted only when the bytes are ICO, PNG, GIF, WebP, JPEG or SVG, and returned as the same base64 data URL. It keeps one reading per origin (twelve hours for an icon, thirty minutes for a site without one, 500 origins and 8 MB, oldest first) and shares a read already in flight, so devices asking together cost one request. A shared server must never read its own network on a link's behalf: every host is resolved before its request and private, loopback, link-local, carrier-NAT, multicast and IPv4-mapped addresses are refused, and redirects are followed by hand, at most three, with the same check on each hop. `GET /v1/icon/<encoded origin>` answers `{"icon": …}` behind the caller's own browser session or pairing key, with `Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600`; only the origin reaches the site, never the page a reply linked to, and the route reads no workspace data. `transport.siteIcon` uses it whenever the app is not the desktop app, leaving the marks, their bounds and the globe fallback unchanged, and the same 8 MB budget now bounds the host's readings as well. Until a release updates the production relay, that route answers 404 there and the Viewer keeps showing the globe.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 403 unit/HTTP tests in 61 files, production build, 247 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 299 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/link-marks/verify.log` and `rust.log`. New coverage: `site-icons.test.ts` reads the usual address before a declared icon, refuses a page served as an icon, an oversized icon and a page read past its limit, refuses a private address before any request and a redirect into one, keeps one reading per site with its expiry, shares a read in flight, makes room for large icons, and drives the route through a real relay for a paired browser (the icon, its cache header, only the origin asked, 400 for addresses that are not sites and for a malformed escape, 401 without a session and 403 from another device). `tests/markdown.spec.ts` adds a Viewer scenario: the seeded reply's Wikipedia mark takes the icon its relay read, the relative link stays plain, prose holds no image element, and the phone-width layout keeps the link visible. The Rust `keeps_readings_until_they_expire_and_makes_room_for_new_origins` now also covers the byte budget. Screenshot: `artifacts/link-marks-viewer.png`. Not exercised: the production relay and a physical phone, and a real site read from the relay, whose reader is driven by an injected fetcher.
## Site marks on links — 2026-09-26

The user asked for link rendering like Codex's: the linked site's own icon before the link text. Links in replies, reasoning, progress comments and proposed plans now open with a mark, and the link itself is medium weight in the accent color with its underline only on hover. `siteLink` in `src/lib/link-marks.ts` gives every address a browser would open as a page one class per site, 500 at a time, and the Marked link renderer in `markdown.ts` emits `<a href><span class="link-mark site-N"></span>text</a>`; every other address, and anything Marked itself rejects, keeps its plain anchor. `.link-mark` in `styles.css` masks a globe in the link's own color, so an unread, unknown or unreachable icon still holds exactly the space the icon will take. The computer showing the reply reads each site once and a single inserted stylesheet rule (`.link-mark.site-N`) replaces the globe, so a streaming reply never walks its links again and saved history, tool activity and proposed plans are marked by the same renderer without a change in their components.

`site_icons.rs` reads the icon on that computer: `/favicon.ico` on the site's own origin and, when that serves none, the beginning of its page (256 KB) for the icons it declares, trying up to three. Only `rel` values naming an icon count, so a stylesheet or Safari's `mask-icon` is skipped, and a reading is accepted only when its first bytes are ICO, PNG, GIF, WebP, JPEG or SVG — developer.mozilla.org serves a PNG as `image/x-icon`, and the HTML many sites return for a missing icon must never become a mark. Icons are bounded at 256 KB and returned as one base64 data URL, which the window CSP (`img-src 'self' data:`) already allows. Readings stay in a process-local map (twelve hours for an icon, thirty minutes for a site without one, 500 origins with the oldest evicted) and the page reads four at a time, so a reply full of links never opens a burst of connections. Nothing about a link is stored or sent on: no workspace field, export, prompt replay or relay message carries a mark, and only the origin, never the page or its path, reaches the site. Browser clients kept the generic mark until the entry above gave the relay a reader of its own.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 394 unit/HTTP tests in 60 files, production build, 246 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 299 Rust tests with eleven opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/link-marks/verify.log` and `rust.log`. New coverage: `link-marks.test.ts` (one mark per site across its pages, another scheme, host or port as another site, plain links for mail, `javascript:`, data, file, relative and invalid addresses; a rule only for bounded base64 image data, refusing other media types, an unencoded address, one that would close the rule and reach outside it, and an oversized one), the Rust `keeps_only_the_origin_of_an_http_link`, `recognizes_icon_bytes_and_rejects_a_page_served_instead`, `reads_the_icons_a_page_declares_in_order`, `reads_an_attribute_without_confusing_its_name` and `keeps_readings_until_they_expire_and_makes_room_for_new_origins`, and a `tests/markdown.spec.ts` scenario covering the marked and unmarked links of one reply, one read per origin, the icon replacing the globe, the globe keeping its size for a site that serves none, no image element in prose, the link opening outside the app, and a restored reply marked again from its saved Markdown. Screenshot: `artifacts/link-marks.png`.

Real sites: the opt-in `reads_the_icons_real_sites_serve` read en.wikipedia.org, github.com and docs.rs (ICO) and developer.mozilla.org (PNG), kept the Wikipedia reading for a second link, and returned none for `example.invalid`. In a Playwright page, Chromium decoded Wikipedia's 2,734-byte ICO as a data URL at 48×48, so the format most sites still serve renders in WebView2. Not exercised: the native app, since the change is Svelte rendering plus one command with its own tests; the Viewer, which keeps the generic mark; and a site that answers slowly or refuses an unknown user agent, where the globe remains.
## File type icons in Files edited — 2026-09-26

The user asked for each edited file to show the icon of its type. Every row of Files edited now starts with a quiet icon beside its chevron, chosen by `fileTypeIcon` in `src/lib/tool-presentation.ts` from the file name alone: a stylesheet gets a palette, markup the angle brackets, a Svelte, Vue or JSX component the component mark, JSON data braces, configuration a cogged page, a shell script a terminal page, a database a cylinder, a spreadsheet a grid, a font the type mark, and images, media, archives, binaries and notebooks their own; every other language shares the code page. The name decides before the extension, so a lock file (`Cargo.lock`, `package-lock.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `bun.lockb`), a Dockerfile or compose file, a Git file, a Makefile or other build file and a test (`.test.*`, `.spec.*`, `_test.go`, `test_*.py`) keep an icon of their own, and dot-files such as `.env.production` or `.prettierrc` count as configuration. An unrecognized name keeps the plain document icon, as Markdown and text do. The icons come from the existing `ToolIcon` vocabulary, extended with thirteen keys that only file rows use, so tool calls and their group icons are unchanged. They are decorative: each row still carries its path, the full path on hover, and its Added or Edited label.

New coverage: `tool-presentation.test.ts` checks 38 paths across every icon, a Windows path with an upper-case extension, and that a name such as `unlock.ts` is not read as a lock file. `tests/file-changes.spec.ts` now gives two of its extra files a `.css` and a `.html` name and checks the component, document, stylesheet and markup icons of the rows at desktop and phone widths. ## Notifications name their chat — 2026-09-25

The user asked for notifications to show a real title and description from the chat that stopped instead of a generic message. Desktop cards and phone pushes said Reply ready, Your agent stopped and the like with a fixed line, as the notification rules required. `chatNotification` in `src/lib/notifications.ts` now titles each alert with the chat's title (the first message until a title is generated) and adds one line about the reply: the start of the final answer, or of a proposed plan when that is the answer; `Stopped:` and the text a stopped reply had reached, from its answer or its latest progress comment, skipping Agent Studio's own `studio-` session notices; `Failed:` and the error without a leading `Error:`; or `Question:` and the pending question, and `<server> requests your input.` for MCP input. `markdownText` reads Markdown as one plain line without a parser, since the relay image installs only web-push and zod: code blocks (indented in list items or unclosed while streaming too), comments and visualization markers are dropped; code spans and escaped characters stay literal; link targets, image addresses, HTML tags and emphasis markers go; headings, list items, table rows and paragraphs join with `: ` or ` · ` unless they end a sentence, and reading stops once there is twice the line's length. `notificationLine` removes control characters, noncharacters and lone surrogates, which native IPC or toast XML would reject, and cuts at a word within 80 code points for titles and 180 for the line. Without such text an alert keeps the generic line of its kind, and an untitled chat the generic title.

The desktop tracker attaches the text to each notice. The native `Notice` takes optional `title` and `body` (still `deny_unknown_fields`; refused on test notices and above 1,000 and 2,000 bytes), cleans them again for toast XML and falls back to the generic text. The Windows toast library escapes XML itself; on Linux the line is escaped when the notification server reports `body-markup`, a path reviewed against notify-rust 4.18 but not compiled here, as this host has no Linux toolchain (the Linux release runner builds it). The relay builds the same text in `changed` from the workspace checkpoint, and in `jobUpdated` for a remote reply that ends before its final checkpoint arrives: from the job's retained events (whose latest `text` event holds the whole answer, plus progress comments, proposed plans and `job.error`) and a new `conversationTitle` lookup in the relay's copy of the workspace. Its `noticeSchema` keeps the text in queued deliveries until they are delivered or expire. The service worker passes received text through `notificationContent`, which cleans and bounds it again and keeps test notifications generic. Settings now says alerts show the chat's title and the start of its reply, and on phones that the notification preview settings decide whether the lock screen shows it. NOTIFICATIONS.md, MOBILE.md and the notification rules in CLAUDE.md describe the new content.

Fixed on the way: the native side accepted only `<run>:attention` and `<run>:terminal` tags, so a second question call in a reply (`<run>:attention:<id>`) or an MCP input request (`<run>:elicitation:<id>`) never showed a desktop alert. It now accepts those keys with a UUID.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 391 unit/HTTP tests in 59 files, production build, 244 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 294 Rust tests with ten opt-in cases ignored. One earlier full run timed out in `failures offer retry and stop keeps partial output` before its page rendered; it passed in the other three runs, including the final one. Logs: `artifacts/notification-content/verify.log` and `rust.log`. New coverage: `notifications.test.ts` (Markdown read as one line: headings, lists, fenced code inside list items, tables, links, images, escapes, code spans, entities, HTML tags, unclosed fences and the reading budget; cutting at a word by code point with emoji; control characters and lone surrogates; every kind's line and fallbacks; the question each attention key names; received text bounded and tests kept generic), `desktop-notifications.test.ts` (notices carry the chat title and reply line), `push.test.ts` (checkpoint alerts carry the title and flattened reply and leave no text in `web-push.json` once delivered; remote jobs described from their events, with a deleted chat's generic title and a question) and the Rust `shows_the_chat_title_and_reply_line_as_plain_bounded_text` plus UUID-named attention tags in `accepts_only_bounded_lifecycle_notices`. Updated: `relay-workspaces.test.ts` now checks that Alice's device gets her chat's text and Bob's never does, and `tests/desktop-notifications.spec.ts` and `tests/notifications.spec.ts` check the delivered title and line and the worker's notification. Native: `scripts/notification-content-native-smoke.mjs` drove the isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.notification-content-qa` build (`scripts/native-notification-content.tauri.json`, CDP 19741, cold scratch target 59 s) with the chime muted, reading each toast back from Windows' notification history for that identity. ## History grouped by app session — 2026-09-25

The user asked for History to be grouped into sessions: one run of the app, from opening Agent Studio to closing it, titled with the day and time the app started. Each desktop start records `{id, environmentId, startedAt}` in the optional synced workspace field `appSessions` (`src/lib/app-sessions.ts`). The id and time come from the native `app_session` command, captured once per process, so window reloads, which also move chats to History, stay in the same session. A History chat belongs to the latest recorded start at or before its newest message (or its creation, for an unsent fork), so continuing an older chat moves it to the current session once it is back in History; chats last used before the first recorded start are grouped by day, with a calendar icon instead of a clock. `groupConversations` in `src/lib/locations.ts` now returns sections: Active keeps computer then folder, and History lists sessions, newest first, then their folders, which keep their New conversation actions. Folders name their computer when a session holds chats of several, and headings name the computer that started the app only when several have. Headings read Today, Yesterday, a weekday within the last week, or the date, followed by the start time, and give the full date on hover. Each start drops that installation's earlier sessions without chats (never another computer's, whose chats may still be syncing) and keeps the newest 1,000. Sync merges the list by id against the base. Relays and apps released before this drop the field, so a side without it counts as unaware of it rather than empty: this device keeps its own list, `sameShared` compares the list only when the relay's copy has one, and `pollRelay` sends it only with another change while the relay lacks it, so an older client removing it cannot start a write loop. The production relay drops it until a release updates it, so a paired development build shows sessions from its own list and the Viewer shows them after that release.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 377 unit/HTTP tests in 58 files, production build, 244 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 293 Rust tests with ten opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/history-sessions/verify.log` and `rust.log`.
