## Workspace saves while commands read it — 2026-09-25

While verifying History sessions natively, the isolated QA app showed "Could not load your workspace. Cannot finish workspace save. The previous file was preserved." after a page reload. `write_workspace` replaced `workspace.json` with tempfile's `persist`, which calls `MoveFileExW(MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING)`. Many commands read `workspace.json` without the storage lock (`saved::fleet` for connection checks, models and usage, `run_agent`'s history check, native instructions, tool output pruning), and Windows refuses that replace while any handle has the file open, even one opened with delete sharing: a Node probe got EPERM while a reader was open and succeeded after it closed. A startup save overlaps the connection and model checks that startup begins at the same time. A build of c2ab837 driven through a fresh profile, a seeded chat, a page reload and a relaunch failed after the reload in one of three runs; the History sessions build, whose startup awaits one more native call, failed four times in four. `replace_workspace` now keeps the flushed temporary file, which clears its temporary attribute, and renames it with `std::fs::rename`, which on Windows 10 1607 and later retries a refused replace with POSIX semantics, so a reader keeps the previous file. It deletes the temporary file when the rename fails. Undo's receipt save uses the same helper. The rebuilt QA app passed the same probe three times in three and the History sessions native smoke.

Validation: the Rust test `replaces_the_workspace_while_a_command_reads_it` holds a reader open across the replace; it failed with tempfile's `persist` and passes now. The pipeline in the History sessions entry covers both changes.

## Move to history from the sidebar — 2026-09-25

The user asked for Move to history on hovered sidebar chats, for the toolbar's Move to history to be its first button, and for the app to continue with the next Active chat instead of staying on the moved one. Each sidebar row is now a `.conversation-row` holding its `.conversation-item` and, for Active chats whose reply is not running, a `.conversation-archive` button named "Move <title> to history". It fades in on row hover or keyboard focus, shows at 44 px on phones and always under `hover: none`, and the title ellipsizes before it while it shows. `archiveConversation` in `+page.svelte` now takes the conversation. When it is the open chat, `nextActiveConversation` in `src/lib/locations.ts` picks the Active conversation listed below it in the sidebar's grouping, else the one above it. While searching it looks in the listed matches first, then in all Active chats, and a new chat opens when none is left. The sidebar stays on Active instead of revealing History. A row's action keeps any other open chat, its draft, the view and the phone drawer, and keyboard focus moves to the row now in its place. From the toolbar, focus continues in the composer, or at the actions menu on phones.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 364 unit/HTTP tests in 57 files, production build, 236 browser scenarios) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Log: `artifacts/move-to-history/verify.log`. New coverage: `locations.test.ts` (next conversation across folders and computers, above at the end, History and scratch chats skipped) and `tests/move-to-history.spec.ts` (toolbar order; the next, previous and new chat after the toolbar action; search matches first; the hover button hidden at rest, row geometry, the open chat and draft kept; no button in History or while replying; keyboard focus; phones keep the drawer open). Updated scenarios: the Gemini sign-out check in `workspace.spec.ts` reopens the archived chat from History, the folder-groups check expects a new chat with Active kept, `forks.spec.ts` opens History before focusing the archived row, and `elicitations.spec.ts` and `questions.spec.ts` anchor their row-title regexes, which also matched the new buttons' names. Screenshots: `artifacts/move-to-history-hover.png` and `artifacts/move-to-history-phone.png`; zoomed dark and light captures of hover, keyboard focus and a long title were reviewed during development. No native check: the change is frontend-only, and the scenarios ran in Edge, the engine of WebView2.

## Remembered agent — 2026-09-25

New chats, including the one opened at startup, started with the account of the reply that started last rather than the account last chosen. `rememberSettings` stored the provider and account together with the model and reasoning on every send and retry and on every settings change, in any conversation. Replying in an older chat on another account, or changing its model, reasoning, Plan mode, Fast mode or instructions, therefore moved the default. On 2026-09-25 a reply on vinporb, the Claude account of the terminal login, made the app reopen on it after the update restart, and the user cancelled the next chat's first message to send it again on viniciusportelab. `rememberAgent` in `src/lib/domain.ts` now records the provider and account separately, and only for choices: the Agent picker for a new chat or for an existing chat's next reply, **Chat** in Connections, and `/new`. `rememberSettings` still keeps the model per provider and the reasoning per provider and model from any chat. The startup chat, **New conversation**, the sidebar **+** and Ctrl+N use the chosen agent, and version 1 migration still derives both from saved agents and chats.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 363 unit/HTTP tests in 57 files, production build, 231 browser scenarios) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Log: `artifacts/remembered-agent/verify.log`. New coverage: `domain.test.ts` (settings changes and replies on other accounts keep the chosen agent and account; a choice without an account keeps the remembered one) and `tests/remembered-agent.spec.ts` (after a reply and a reasoning change in a chat on the CLI login, **New conversation** and a reload still start on the chosen second account; choosing an account for an existing chat's next reply becomes the new default). On the previous code that test fails at its first check, where the new chat showed the CLI login.

Native: an isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.agent-memory-qa` build (scratch target) was seeded through `save_workspace` with a copy of the user's workspace, its computer and environment IDs mapped to the QA installation. Before the fix, reloads and a title-bar close and relaunch already reopened on the remembered account, so startup itself was not at fault. With the fix, the account chosen for the new chat (viniciusportelab) stayed remembered after its vinporb chat was opened from History and its reasoning changed from Max to High, and the relaunched app opened the new chat on **Claude · viniciusportelab** (`artifacts/remembered-agent/native-startup.png`). No message was sent. The copy held no credentials for separate profiles, so they showed as signed out.

## Scratch chats — 2026-09-25

A new conversation kept one draft per folder or Standalone location, so the sidebar **+** reopened that draft instead of starting another. Every new chat is now a scratch chat with its own id (`src/lib/drafts.ts`): its draft is saved under `scratch:<id>` with the computer, folder and chat settings chosen for it, and `groupConversations` in `src/lib/locations.ts` lists the ones with content first in their Active folder. `+page.svelte` creates one in `newChat()`, keeps the open one's place and settings through an effect, drops it when it is left empty, sent (it becomes the conversation) or discarded from its row's context menu, continues the one edited last when a session starts, and turns `folder:`/`computer:` drafts saved by earlier releases into scratch chats on load. A folder or computer change now moves the scratch chat instead of merging drafts, so `combineDrafts` is gone. Typing only rewrites the open row's title; the sidebar groups are rebuilt when a scratch chat gains or loses its content.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 362 unit/HTTP tests in 57 files, production build, 230 browser scenarios) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. The first full run also failed `tests/input-templates.spec.ts`, which expects a new chat's text back in the composer after a reload; continuing the scratch chat edited last restores that.

## Queued messages send in the background — 2026-09-25

A conversation that is not open now sends its next queued message when its own reply completes, instead of waiting to be opened. `send()` was split: the composer part validates the draft, and `startReply()` starts and follows a reply for both the composer and the background queue. The background path uses the composer's readiness checks for a saved chat (account ready, computer online, relay paired, no reply or stop in progress). It records the reply's model name from the cached catalog and leaves remembered model choices, the open chat's draft and its scroll position alone. A stopped or failed reply, queued mentions from another account, or images that no longer fit the 20 MB workspace keep the queue until the conversation is opened, where the messages return to its draft. Previously a queued message that no longer fit was dropped when it was due to send; it now returns to the composer with the rest of its queue. A finished reply scrolls only its own conversation, so a background reply no longer trims held space in the open chat.

Tests: `tests/concurrent-chats.spec.ts` sends a queued message in a chat that is not open when its reply completes. It keeps the open chat's unsent draft, shows the follow-up and the new running reply when that chat is opened, and keeps a stopped background reply's queue until the chat is opened, when the message returns to the draft. The background-send test failed against the previous page. Validation: `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied, 291 Rust tests, and `npm run verify` (0 Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 360 unit tests in 57 files, the production build and 229 browser tests). Logs: `artifacts/queued-background-rust.log` and `queued-background-verify.log`.

Native: `scripts/concurrent-chats-native-smoke.mjs` now also queues a follow-up in the Codex chat while it runs. The three chats again replied at the same time, the quick Claude reply finished while the others ran, and stopping the long Claude chat left Codex running. With the long chat still open, the Codex reply finished with `CODEX DONE` after 43 s and its queued follow-up went out by itself, answering `CODEX FOLLOW-UP DONE` in 1.7 s. Evidence: `artifacts/concurrent-chats/native-report.json`, `codex-follow-up-sent.png` and `artifacts/concurrent-chats-native.log`.

## Conversations reply side by side — 2026-09-25

Only one reply could run at a time. The native run registry (`runner::Runs`) refused any second reply with `Another response is still running`, and the page kept a single `run` slot and a check for replies running on this computer. Together they disabled Send and the new-chat pickers in every other conversation behind an "An agent is responding in another conversation. View it" banner. Admission is now per conversation: one reply or file Undo per conversation, and a stopped reply keeps its conversation's slot until its process exits, so the reload recovery below still waits for the old provider. Other conversations are admitted immediately, whether started locally or through relay jobs. The page tracks runs, stopping and steering by conversation. Stop, deletion and steering act only on their own conversation, and the new-chat pickers, history actions, composer commands and Model context management depend on the open conversation. Connections sign-in and focus refreshes still wait for every local reply. Title generation stays limited to two at a time per computer; a third chat started with them now retries for up to two minutes instead of keeping its excerpt. Queued messages still dispatch only for the open conversation.

Tests: the Rust registry tests now cover two conversations running at once, a second reply refused in the same conversation, and the reload regressions scoped per conversation, with another conversation admitted while a stopped one cleans up. `tests/concurrent-chats.spec.ts` runs two chats at once, checks that Stop sends `cancel_run` only for the open chat's run, and checks that a queued message waits for its own conversation's reply. Both browser tests failed against the previous page. Validation: `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied, 291 Rust tests, and `npm run verify` (0 Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 360 unit tests in 57 files, the production build and 228 browser tests). Logs: `artifacts/concurrent-chats-rust.log` and `concurrent-chats-verify.log`.

Native: an isolated build (`scripts/native-concurrent-chats.tauri.json`, CDP 19711) driven by `scripts/concurrent-chats-native-smoke.mjs` sent three real chats within 40 ms: two Claude Sonnet chats on the same existing login and one Codex GPT-5.6 Sol chat, each in its own disposable folder. All three showed as running together. The quick Claude reply finished in 1.8 s while the other Claude chat was running `sleep 60`. Stop then cancelled that chat mid-command while Codex kept running, and Codex finished with `CODEX DONE` after 46 s. No page errors occurred. The driver seeds its chats again when an app save from startup detection overwrites them. Evidence: `artifacts/concurrent-chats/native-report.json`, `three-chats-running.png`, `codex-finished-after-stop.png` and `artifacts/concurrent-chats-native.log`. ## Tool calls show commands, outputs and images — 2026-09-25

Tool calls now say what ran and what came back. Records show the command a shell call ran (Codex's `powershell.exe -Command '…'` and `bash -lc '…'` wrappers unwrapped, following the quoting the installed Codex 0.153.4 app-server was observed to use: single quotes unless the script contains one, then escaped double quotes) and arguments of connected and unrecognized tools. Results stay on the executing computer complete, with no size, count or age limit, and are read when a call is opened; the synced record keeps only their size and exit code. Each call and group has an icon for what it does, including a Lucide-style Git logo for git commands.

Why the results stay out of the workspace: it is one JSON document, capped at 20 MB, that is saved whole after every change and synced whole. Measurements of this machine's Claude Code transcripts from the last 20 days (numbers only) found 35,078 shell calls averaging 2.2 KB of output (99th percentile 18 KB), 7,708 reads and 4,668 images averaging about 180 KB, 4,492 of them from Read: a few days of results would pass the cap, and every save and sync would carry them. The first version of the store kept bounded results (256 KB per stream, eight images, 30 days, 2 GB); at the user's request it now keeps everything as sent, one folder per call with `stdout.txt`, `stderr.txt`, image files and `meta.json`, until the chat is deleted. Windows receive each stream whole up to 512 KB, or its ends with **Show full output** for up to 8 MB per stream, so a full view fits one 20 MB relay request, and images one at a time. A probe also showed that this machine's Codex PreToolUse hook prepends `node "…/hooks/claude-context/context-hook.cjs" --record-cwd <hash>` to every command, so rows name the first line after such hook lines and details show them quietly.

Validation: `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied and 291 Rust tests (decoder fixtures for Claude shell success, failure exit codes, `returnCodeInterpretation`, git operations, image reads, MCP text and images, web pages, 5 MB outputs, 20-image results and commands longer than the record keeps; Codex wrapped commands with hook prefixes, CRLF and ANSI output, image views, failed MCP calls and dynamic tools; the store's image sniffing and dimensions, raw streams with cleaned previews, ends of long streams on line and character boundaries, full views, image reads, copied image files, in-flight reads, request validation, and pruning only unreferenced runs whatever their age or size); `npm run check`; unit tests (command classification, row visuals, result and image schemas, fetch caches, relay `toolOutput` and `toolOutputImage` jobs); the production build; and the full browser suite, including `tests/tool-output.spec.ts` on desktop and phone widths (icons, commands, outputs, standard error, exit codes, numbered file content, images loaded on their own with a preview, **Show full output** for a 3,000-line log, a shortened command shown whole, retry after a failed read, no request for empty or unopened results, inert HTML, saved history without outputs, reload). Four existing scenarios changed with the policy: background work now asserts that the list shows descriptions while the call shows its command, and the long-chat scroll fixture gives its calls commands, since calls without recorded commands have single-line rows. Running them exposed a real issue, fixed before commit: `overscroll-behavior: contain` on result boxes stopped the chat from scrolling while the pointer was over a box that could not scroll.

Native: an isolated build (`scripts/native-tool-output.tauri.json`, CDP 19703) driven by `scripts/tool-output-native-smoke.mjs` ran real Claude Sonnet and Codex GPT-5.6 Sol replies on 2026-09-25 in disposable git folders, before and after the change to complete storage. Each ran `git status --short`, read `notes.txt` and viewed a 16×8 red and blue PNG. Both saved the command (Claude as Bash; Codex as PowerShell with its hook line), output sizes and exit code 0 without any output text in the record; `read_tool_output` returned the whole git output and file text, `read_tool_output_image` the 16×8 PNG, and each call's folder under `tool-output/<run>/` held its streams, `image-0.png` (Codex's copied from the viewed file) and `meta.json`; the opened calls showed the Git icon, command, output, numbered file content and the image. Both models named the colors. Evidence: `artifacts/tool-output/native-report.json` and `{claude,codex}-native-{command,image}.png`. The first native run showed Claude Code writing notes such as "Shell cwd was reset…" to standard error, so a successful call labels it **Standard error** in neutral colors, and tiny images are enlarged with crisp pixels. Not exercised: WSL image-view paths, Viewer and cross-computer reads through a live relay (covered by the relay job tests), connected tools returning images live, outputs above 8 MB from a real provider, and other operating systems. Until a release updates the relay, a development build paired with the production relay loses the new saved fields once sync adopts the relay's copy, because the relay validates saved chats with its own schema; live replies still show them, and reads through the relay need its `toolOutput` and `toolOutputImage` job methods.

## Guards against freezes — 2026-09-24

Today's three freezes, recorded below (opening Connections, relay polls, and selecting text in long chats), came from three patterns: whole-workspace copies on hot paths, file work in synchronous native commands on the UI thread, and collapsed content kept in the page. Tests now fail when any of them returns, and [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md) collects the rules, the guards, their budgets and the profiling recipe. The five native commands that still read or wrote files synchronously, `load_workspace`, `load_sync_state`, `export_workspace`, `get_installation` and `save_artifact`, now run on the blocking pool as the saves already did. The only synchronous commands left return from memory: the pending-chat badge, live account updates, background work, title cancellation and the update status.

`src/lib/performance-guards.test.ts` parses the frontend with the TypeScript compiler and names every whole-workspace copy by file and enclosing functions. Today there are 13: the runtime's getter, saves, relay merges after either side changed, relay adoption, Undo edits, forks, the size check before sending images, and exports. The list must match an allowlist that gives a reason for each entry. The test also requires every synchronous Tauri command to be on its own allowlist, after checking that the scan found every command registered in `generate_handler!`, and it keeps the closed `<details>` rule. Self-checks run the detectors on synthetic code. `tests/performance.spec.ts` loads a generated 2.43 MB workspace shaped like the heaviest local one (2.48 MB): 24 chats, the longest with 34 messages, 64 replies with tool activity, progress comments, reasoning and recorded diffs, and 12 ready connections. A desktop mock pairs it with a real in-process relay and serializes each save as IPC does. One scenario, run everywhere including CI, checks that eight idle seconds after the first sync send heartbeats and revision probes, never download or upload `v1/state`, and save nothing. The other checks that Connections shows every account, that the longest chat keeps fewer than 500 elements inside collapsed disclosures, and that a selection covers it. Outside CI it also holds the longest main-thread task to 100 ms while idle, 250 ms opening Connections and 150 ms while dragging a selection and pressing Ctrl+A.

The budgets come from the development server in headless Edge 153. The current code had no task over 50 ms while idle or selecting, 52–62 ms opening Connections, and 34 elements inside collapsed disclosures (2,016 in the page). In a scratch copy I reintroduced each fixed cause in turn:

- Polls that always fetched the whole state failed the idle scenario, which listed the `GET v1/state` requests.
- Polls that also applied and saved it failed both scenarios, with a task of 367–435 ms on every poll.
- Fleet lookups copying the whole workspace failed the guard, which named `src/routes/+page.svelte › onMount callback › fleet`, and held Connections for one 1,891 ms task.
- Rendering collapsed content up front put 8,517 elements inside closed disclosures.
- Removing the `::details-content` rule failed the guard.

Even with collapsed content rendered up front and the rule removed, no selection task reached 50 ms. The style fixes since the selection freeze made those hidden elements cheap, so the element count, not the timing, catches that regression.

Validation: `npm run verify` passed in full on the final candidate in an isolated copy (port 1437, its own Vite cache). It reported zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 349 unit/HTTP tests in 55 files, the production build and 223 browser scenarios, with both new scenarios running beside the other workers. The copy's first full run, on the previous tip with a fresh Vite cache, stalled two page loads while dependencies were optimized: `app-updates.spec.ts:50`, a known first-run case, and `input-templates.spec.ts:115`, which never saw the toolbar. Its browser suite then passed in full. `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied and 279 Rust tests (10 opt-in cases ignored) passed on the final candidate.

Native Windows check: an isolated debug build (`com.vinicius.agentstudio.async-commands-qa`, scratch Cargo target) launched through `npm run start:windows -Executable`. It started normally and returned its installation identity, workspace and empty checkpoint in 3–4 ms. It rejected an invalid export and artifact with their usual messages and wrote a real export and artifact to Downloads. The check then deleted those files, the QA data and the build.

The browser checks ran in a copy because the development server on port 1420 answered its page with HTTP 500, a stale dependency pre-bundle. Playwright then started a second server in the same checkout, which rewrote the shared `node_modules/.vite` before stopping on the busy port. Not exercised: real provider runs, which this change does not touch.

## Native lookups parse only what they check — 2026-09-24

Commands acting for one account connection (usage reads, detection, model lists, sign-in, titles, Model context, mentions, MCP, plugins and replies) parsed all of `workspace.json` into a `serde_json::Value` to read the account registry: in `profiles::resolve`, twice for a connection sharing another account's context, and again in `folders::validate_chat` and `folders::environment_distribution`. `run_agent`'s stale-history check, `undo_files` and the Native prompt tab parsed it for a few conversation fields. `src-tauri/src/saved.rs` now deserializes typed structs that hold only `fleet`, or each conversation's id, history revision, settings, location and message role, status and run, so serde skips the history without building it. Commands still read the saved file on every call, and a malformed file or invalid UTF-8 anywhere in it is still rejected. The connection, environment-ownership and managed WSL checks are unchanged, and a context source resolves from its connection's read with its id still checked.

On a synthetic 2.50 MB workspace shaped like the local 2.49 MB one (29 chats, 132 messages, about 46,000 strings, `conversations` saved before `fleet`), built with the app's serde_json 1.0.151 features and profiles, one read went from 37 to 15 ms in a debug build and from 6.1 to 1.6 ms in release, with 273 allocations instead of 84,353. `resolve` for a connection sharing another account's context went from 74 to 16 ms in debug and from 13 to 2.3 ms in release, and the lookups before a folder chat's reply (history check, folder check and `resolve`) from 115 to 49 ms and from 19 to 5.8 ms. The app's own test build (34.6 → 14.6 ms per read) and timing alone on the local file (39 → 15 ms in debug, 6–9 → 1.5 ms in release) agree. Most of what remains in debug is serde_json skipping history without optimizations: built at opt-level 3, the same read took 3.5 ms, but the dev profile is unchanged. A chat command still reads the file once per check, two to four times.

Validation: `npm run verify` passed on the final candidate (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 345 unit/HTTP tests in 54 files, production build, 221 browser scenarios), with `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied and 279 Rust tests (ten opt-in cases ignored). An earlier run of the same code failed only `app-updates.spec.ts:50` on its cold Vite server, the known first-load flake. New tests in `saved.rs` compare the typed reads with a whole parse, including `"fleet"` text and a nested `fleet` key inside history, and keep missing, truncated, invalid-token, bad-escape, control-character, invalid-UTF-8 and trailing-data files unreadable. A `profiles.rs` test resolves a context source from the same registry and refuses a saved source id such as `../escape` before it names a profile directory. The benchmark and the fixture generator stayed in the session scratchpad; the local workspace was only timed, and nothing from it was committed.

## Opening Connections without copying the workspace — 2026-09-24

Opening Connections froze the app for about a second. Every request routed to an account or computer (usage reads, account management, model lists, folder browsing, Model context, file mentions) found its target in `remoteTarget`. That called `runtime.workspace()`, a `$state.snapshot` of the whole workspace, twice just to read the fleet. Connections reads usage for every signed-in account and requests account details, so it copied a 2.4 MB workspace about nine times in one task. The same full copy served other fleet-only lookups: account heartbeats (twice per live account-usage update, on every 2.5-second relay poll), received account updates, the context cache key, and the ownership checks of jobs this computer runs for other devices, inside a `find` callback and so once per connection. The runtime now offers `fleet()`, a snapshot of `workspace.fleet` alone, and all of these read it. Only syncing, conflict resolution and exports still copy the whole workspace.

In headless Edge 153 with the local workspace, the desktop mock and an emulated paired relay, opening Connections was one 930–980 ms long task, with the view after about a second. Now it takes 38–65 ms, with no long tasks, and 35–119 ms of main-thread work over the next five seconds instead of 1,007–1,397 ms. A CPU profile had attributed 901 ms to `remoteTarget`'s snapshots: 697 ms from usage reads and 206 ms from account management. On isolated native QA builds (WebView2, the 2.49 MB workspace, real CLI detection and usage reads), the committed code blocked the page for 461–550 ms and showed the view after 503–621 ms; the change shows it after 32–101 ms with no long tasks. The QA copy had fewer signed-in accounts than the development app, since its separate profiles have no logins, and each ready account adds snapshots. A probe sending `WM_NULL` to the window every 20 ms saw the UI thread answer within 6–8 ms throughout, in both builds, so the stall was in the page.

Validation: `npm run verify` passed on the final candidate (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 345 unit/HTTP tests in 54 files, production build, 221 browser scenarios, all on the first run) and `cargo fmt --check`; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Log: `artifacts/selection-freeze/verify-5.log`. `src/lib/transport.test.ts` now routes a usage read and polls with a peer's live account update in every heartbeat, counting whole-workspace reads. It expects none; the previous transport made two for the usage read. The profiling scripts stayed in the scratchpad and `artifacts/selection-freeze/` and printed only timings and counts; the QA data and builds were deleted afterwards. Not addressed: the native side still parses the whole `workspace.json` in `profiles::resolve` for each connection-scoped command. That costs CPU on background threads, noticeable in debug builds, but does not block the window.

## Relay polls without whole-workspace work — 2026-09-24

Selecting text still froze small chats after the fix below. The cause was the paired relay poll, which runs every 2.5 seconds whatever chat is open. Each poll copied the whole workspace with `$state.snapshot` three times (twice for the merge and once more to save), validated it with zod, merged and serialized both copies, applied the result to every conversation and saved it. The native side then wrote `workspace.json` and `sync-state.json`, about 2.49 MB each, flushing both to disk on the UI thread, which also handles the window's input. `save_workspace` also reread and parsed the previous file on every save, to check for a pre-migration backup. On the reporting machine both files were rewritten every 2.5 seconds while the app sat idle, with unchanged content. In headless Edge 153 with the local workspace and an in-process relay, a small chat (817 elements) spent 2,907 ms of every 10 idle seconds on the main thread (8 long tasks, up to 332 ms), and dragging a selection had a step of 864 ms. Unpaired, the same chat spent 2 ms, with a slowest step of 10 ms. A CPU profile attributed about 1.1 s of those 10 seconds to the snapshots, 0.5 s to applying and saving, and 0.2 s to zod and the merge.

The page now counts local changes to shared data (`persist()` and streamed run events, not data that `apply` received from the relay). Once a poll leaves both sides equal, later polls only ask the relay for its revision through the new `GET /v1/state/revision`, and do nothing more until that revision moves or this device changes something. Older relays answer 404; the app then reads the revision from the full state they send, without a local sync. Replies running on this computer still sync every poll, since other devices follow their progress. A poll whose relay holds exactly what it sent no longer applies or saves anything, and the checkpoint is saved only when it changes. Comparing what was sent needed `sameShared`, which compares lists by id: this device adds new chats at the front, while a merge keeps its base order and appends them. On the reporting machine the chats were identical but in reverse order, so a whole-JSON comparison would have kept every poll applying and saving. `save_workspace` and `save_sync_state` now run as async commands on the blocking pool, as `save_drafts` already did, and the pre-migration check runs only on the first save after launch.

Same harness, same small chat, after the change: with the new relay endpoint, 11 ms of main-thread work in 10 idle seconds, no long tasks and no saves, and a slowest drag step of 36 ms (none over 50 ms). With a relay lacking the endpoint, 656 ms, no long tasks and no saves. The remaining cost is receiving the 2.4 MB state. The harness delivers it through the same DevTools channel as its mouse input, which produced one 437 ms drag step without a page long task; in the app that is one parse per poll. On the reporting machine, after the development app reloaded the change, neither file was written again while idle.

Validation: `npm run verify` passed on the final candidate (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 344 unit/HTTP tests in 54 files, production build, 221 browser scenarios, all on the first run), with `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied, and 273 Rust tests (ten opt-in cases ignored). Logs: `artifacts/selection-freeze/verify-4.log` and `rust-tests.log`. New unit tests in `src/lib/transport.test.ts` drive a relay with real revision semantics. They show settled polls asking only for the revision, with no workspace read and no apply, while presence and jobs still run. They also cover a local rename and another device's rename each syncing once and settling, a new chat the relay lists in another order settling without an apply, relays without the endpoint, and replies running here syncing every poll. All five fail against the previous transport. `src/lib/sync.test.ts` covers `sameShared`, and `src/lib/relay.test.ts` covers the endpoint's authentication and revision.

An earlier full run failed four browser scenarios that assumed each poll downloads `/v1/state`. Three wait for a stale Viewer tab's poll to be rejected after its cookie changes. They now also accept `/v1/state/revision`, which the relay rejects the same way in its shared authentication step, so the tab still signs out. The fourth, the PWA artifact scenario, clicked the sandboxed preview within milliseconds of opening it. Chromium can drop input to a freshly rendered cross-origin frame, and the page reached that point sooner once it stopped re-applying the workspace: the previous build passed it three of three times, the new one failed two of three. It now retries the click until the counter changes.

Native: `scripts/relay-idle-native-smoke.mjs` drove an isolated debug build (`scripts/native-relay-idle.tauri.json`, CDP 19694, scratch Cargo target) launched through `npm run start:windows -- -Executable`. The build was seeded with a copy of the local 2.49 MB workspace and paired with an in-process relay. In 15 idle seconds after pairing it made 6 revision requests and no full-state downloads or uploads, rewrote neither file, and logged no long tasks. After Claude chat instructions were saved in Settings, the relay received them and the next 12 idle seconds looked the same. After another device changed them through the relay, the app applied and saved the change, then settled again (5 revision requests, no rewrites, no long tasks). No renderer errors. Disconnecting removed the QA pairing; the QA data, relay data and build were deleted afterwards. Evidence: `artifacts/relay-idle-native/result.json`. The profiling used the scratchpad harness with the local workspace and printed only timings and counts. Not exercised: a real relay deployment, since the VPS installs the endpoint with the next release. Also not exercised: the user's running development app with the new native commands. It still runs the previous binary; with the page change it calls the save commands only on actual changes.

## Selecting text in long chats — 2026-09-24

Selecting reply text in a long conversation could freeze the app for seconds. Chromium hides the contents of a closed `<details>` element with `content-visibility: hidden`: they stay in the page but skip style and layout until something needs them, and a text selection that crosses them does. `Editor::SyncSelection` then restyles every hidden element at once, including contents that were shown before. Each completed reply kept its whole Work history (reasoning, progress comments, tool groups and calls) and its Files edited diffs in the page while collapsed, so in the largest local conversation (34 messages, 537 tool calls) 91% of its 32,807 elements were collapsed content. In a production build in Edge 153, the Chromium of the installed WebView2 runtime, at 2560×1300 and 150% scale, Ctrl+A took about a second (up to 2.9 s in the dev server), nearly all of it one style recalculation of about 13,800 hidden elements. Dragging a selection past the end of the text spent about 28 ms of main-thread work per pointer move, with steps up to 83 ms, because every selection change walks each element in the selected range. Removing the `::selection` rule changed nothing; making closed contents `display: none`, or removing them from the page, removed the recalculation.

Closed `<details>` content is now `display: none` through `::details-content` in `styles.css`, as in other engines, so a selection never restyles hidden content. Chat disclosures also render their contents when first expanded and keep them afterwards: Work history, tool groups and calls, and sub-agent messages and results through `revealedDisclosures` (`src/lib/disclosures.ts`), plus Files edited and each file diff. The summary's click renders the contents before the disclosure opens, so the reading position that `virtual-space.ts` holds measures them; the toggle event, which can arrive after the next frame, covers other ways of opening. Collapsing keeps what rendered, so nested expansion, file scope and list expansion behave as before. Find in page no longer searches collapsed content.

Production builds, same conversation, before and after: the conversation opens in 138–183 ms instead of 346–366 ms, with 2,712 elements instead of 32,775. Ctrl+A takes 13–22 ms instead of 915–956 ms. Thirty pointer moves dragging a selection past the text take 142–217 ms of main-thread work instead of 825–876 ms, and the slowest step 13–33 ms instead of 69–83 ms. First opening the largest Work history takes 22–33 ms instead of 37–53 ms, and the largest tool group 57–77 ms instead of 74–108 ms, because revealing a group no longer styles its hidden tool calls. After every Work history and group has been opened and closed, Ctrl+A takes 27–31 ms instead of 97–99 ms. The selected text is unchanged (36,919 characters from the first answer to the last in both builds). The dev server shows the same pattern: Ctrl+A in 11 ms instead of 1.0–2.9 s.

Validation: `npm run verify` passed on the final candidate (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 336 unit/HTTP tests in 54 files, production build, 221 browser scenarios) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Logs: `artifacts/selection-freeze/verify-2.log`, and `verify.log` for an earlier candidate that failed three scenarios still reading unopened contents. `tests/work-history.spec.ts` now checks that a collapsed live group has no calls in the page, that a completed reply's Work history renders nothing until opened and reports `display: none` for its closed contents, that a group and a call render when expanded, and that collapsing and reopening restores both. It fails against the previous build, where the collapsed group held two tool cards. Six existing scenarios in `background-work`, `subagent-detail`, `file-changes`, `federation` and `workspace` specs read tool calls, sub-agent sections and file panels that were never opened; they now open them first. The file scenario checks that no file changes render before Files edited opens, and the scenario comparing live and saved activity opens every group in both before comparing their sequences. The existing scenario that expands a never-opened group at the end of a running reply still stops following, which confirms that the contents are laid out when the position is held. The profiling loaded the local workspace into the desktop mock in headless Edge, served the production builds through Playwright request routing and printed only timings and counts; its scripts stayed in the session scratchpad. Not exercised: the native app, since the change is style rules and rendering in the same engine; the running development app picked it up through hot reload.

## Exact counts in Work history groups — 2026-09-24

Tool groups in live activity and Work history now say how much happened, not just what kind of work it was: **Read 3 files**, **Ran 2 web searches**, **Edited 2 files, ran 1 command, and read 1 file**. Every kind of action in a group appears with its count, in the order it first ran; the old label named only the first two and ended with "and more". `activityGroupSummary` in `src/lib/activity-groups.ts` counts each reported file path once, however often it was read or edited, so four reads of three files say **Read 3 files** and repeated edits of one file say **Edited 1 file**, matching the reply's Files edited footer. Paths come from the call's reported path, the `Files` fact of Codex file changes and the `Also read` fact of combined skill reads. They are compared with the file-change path key, now exported from `src/lib/file-changes.ts` as `filePathKey`, which ignores separator style and the letter case of Windows paths. A read or edit without a reported path counts as its own file. Sub-agents count once per agent identity, so resuming one does not count it twice. Agent messages and agent-directory checks were previously folded into sub-agent work and now have their own counts. Commands, file and web searches, web pages, skills, images, hooks and other tools count every call. The activity-limit notice is not a call, so its group says that later calls are not shown. Running groups use the progressive form (**Editing 1 file and running 1 command**). A group with a failed, stopped or unconfirmed call keeps nouns that claim no success (**Edits to 1 file and 1 command**, **Reads of 2 files**, **1 command**) beside its outcome. The Work history header still has no counts.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 331 frontend/HTTP tests in 53 files, production build, 216 browser scenarios), `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied, and 269 Rust tests with nine existing opt-in cases ignored (Rust is unchanged). Logs: `artifacts/exact-activity-counts/`. New unit coverage in `src/lib/activity-groups.test.ts` checks that repeated reads and edits of one file count once despite different separators or drive-letter case. It also covers paths from Codex `Files` and skill `Also read` facts, pathless calls counted separately, a resumed sub-agent counted once, messages and directory checks counted apart from sub-agents, every call of the other kinds, the activity-limit notice counted as no call, all kinds listed in first-seen order, and failed groups keeping their noun forms. `tests/work-history.spec.ts` now reads one file a second time and checks that the live label goes from **Read 2 files** to **Read 3 files** while the group holds four calls. It then checks **Editing 1 file and running 1 command** and, after the command fails, **Edits to 1 file and 1 command** with Failed, in saved history and after reload, on desktop and at 390px. `tests/reasoning.spec.ts`, `tests/hooks.spec.ts` and the relay test in `tests/federation.spec.ts` check the counted labels in their timelines. Reviewed the running and saved screenshots (`artifacts/grouped-activity-{running,history}-{desktop,mobile}.png`). Known bound: the activity decoder records at most 12 paths for one Codex file change, so a larger patch counts as 12 files. Not exercised: the native app and real provider runs; the labels use only activity metadata that replies already record.

## App version and changelog in Settings — 2026-09-24

Settings now ends with **About**, on desktop and in the Viewer. It shows the running version (the desktop app's native version from Tauri's `getVersion`, allowed by `core:default`; the Viewer's own build version from `package.json`) and a changelog of every release from the new root `CHANGELOG.md`, newest first. The running release starts open and is marked **Current**; the others are collapsed disclosures, and more than five releases wait behind **Show all releases**. Entries render as inert text with code spans. The changelog loads on demand as its own 2 KB chunk through a `virtual:changelog` Vite plugin: the first version imported `CHANGELOG.md?raw`, which worked in the build but not in the dev server, because Vite checks `?raw` ids, query included, against `server.fs.allow`, which SvelteKit limits to `src` and `node_modules` directories, so the dev server answered with the plain Markdown and the dynamic import failed. Allowing the repository root would have widened dev-server file access, including on the LAN with `TAURI_DEV_HOST`. The relay image copies `CHANGELOG.md` for its frontend build, and the Vitest configuration uses the same plugin. `npm run release:version` now also adds the new version's section, dated today, from the commit subjects since the previous tag (`feat` as Added, `fix` as Fixed, `perf` and other subjects as Changed; build, chore, CI, docs, refactor, style and test commits left out), keeping the file's line endings and any section written beforehand. The release job publishes that section as plain-text GitHub and updater notes through `node scripts/release.ts notes` instead of raw commit subjects. `scripts/release.ts` loads the changelog module only for those commands, because the VPS updater installs that file without the app source. The changelog starts with written entries for 0.2.0 and 0.2.1.

All required checks passed on the final code: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 327 frontend/HTTP tests in 53 files, production build, 216 browser scenarios), `cargo fmt --check`, Clippy with warnings denied, and 269 Rust tests with nine existing opt-in cases ignored. New coverage: `src/lib/changelog.test.ts` (the bundled changelog parses, starts with the app version and loads through the plugin; format errors with line numbers; sections generated from commit subjects with both line endings; hand-written sections kept; release notes; code spans) and `tests/about.spec.ts` (desktop shows the mock's native 0.2.0 with that release open and Current while the newer one opens on request; the built Viewer served by the relay shows its build version as Current and fits 390px without horizontal scrolling). The desktop browser test caught the dev-server failure above. In a scratch clone, `release.ts version patch` added 0.2.2 from a `feat` commit while leaving out a `docs` commit, bumped all five manifests, and `notes` printed the new section. Native Windows check: an isolated debug build (`com.vinicius.agentstudio.about-qa`, native version overridden to 0.2.9, scratch Cargo target) launched through `npm run start:windows -Executable` showed **Agent Studio 0.2.9** over the bundled 0.2.1 frontend, loaded both releases with 0.2.1 open and none Current, and logged no page errors; its data and build were removed afterwards. Reviewed the desktop, Viewer, phone and native screenshots. Not exercised: the release job itself (it runs on the next merge to `main`), the relay container build, and the VPS updater smoke (`update.ts` is unchanged and never loads the changelog).

## Viewer workspace cache in IndexedDB — 2026-09-24

Signing in to the production PWA failed with `QuotaExceededError: Failed to execute 'setItem' on 'Storage'`. The Viewer kept its workspace copy and its sync checkpoint in localStorage, two full copies of the workspace; the owner workspace on the VPS is 2.44 million characters, while localStorage allows about 5 MB per origin (Chromium's 10 MiB quota counts UTF-16 bytes, so about 5 million characters). The failed write aborted sign-in, and every retry repeated it. The workspace copy, checkpoint and backup now live in IndexedDB (database `agent-studio`, store `private-workspaces`) under the same workspace-scoped keys, whose quota is a share of the device's free space. Drafts, the installation identity and the cross-tab session signal stay in localStorage. The first store access on a page moves copies that earlier releases left in localStorage, including the validated unscoped legacy owner copy, and removes them there; a snapshot moves only together with its own checkpoint, and nothing replaces data already in the store. Each write checks its session as its transaction starts, so a save queued by a former session cannot land after sign-out cleared that workspace, and a connection the browser dropped (Safari can close an idle page's IndexedDB connection) is reopened once.

`tests/viewer-storage.spec.ts` reproduced the failure on the previous build: the sign-in page showed the QuotaExceededError for a 5.5 million-character workspace. With the fix it signs in, reloads and restores that workspace with both copies in IndexedDB and none in localStorage, and it moves an earlier release's localStorage cache whose unsynced chat then reaches the server. Unit tests cover migration without replacing newer data or splitting a snapshot from its checkpoint, legacy owner scoping, discarding other workspaces, clearing a workspace's cache and preserving damaged caches. All 62 built-PWA browser tests passed.

## Composer grows with the draft — 2026-09-23

The message input had a fixed three-line height (`rows="3"`; its 200px maximum never applied without automatic sizing), so a longer draft scrolled inside a small box. It now sizes with CSS `field-sizing: content`: three lines at rest, then one more line per line of draft until the whole composer box, the input plus 64px of padding, border and tool row, reaches half the window (`calc(50dvh - 64px)` for the input), after which it scrolls. Phones stop at four lines (`calc(4lh + 2px)`), keeping the conversation visible above the keyboard; the former 100px phone cap would have cut the fourth line by a pixel and shown a scrollbar. Engines without `field-sizing` (before Chromium 123, Safari 26.2 and Firefox 152) keep the fixed three lines. Sizing needs no per-keystroke measuring, which would collapse the field on every keystroke and briefly enlarge the chat, a layout that can clamp the chat's scroll position.

A taller composer shortens the chat from below while the browser keeps its scroll position, so a growing draft pushed the end of a followed conversation out of view: 319px for a 40-line draft in a 900px window. An observer on the conversation viewport now returns a followed chat to its end whenever the viewport gets shorter, whether from a taller composer, a smaller window or a phone keyboard, consuming held virtual space first as streamed updates do. A reading position above the end stays where it is.

The first full run failed two context-meter scenarios in `workspace.spec.ts`: after a reload restored their long test drafts, the opened usage details covered the chat toolbar and intercepted a click on the Model picker. The panel floats above the composer area with a height bound, `clamp(140px, 100dvh - 400px, 480px)`, that assumed a fixed-height composer; in a 900px window with a full composer its top would sit 14px above the window, hiding its Close button. The same observer now publishes the conversation viewport's height to the composer area as `--chat-height`, a small subtree to restyle, and the panel also stays within that height minus 12px, scrolling its own content. With a resting composer the previous bound is the smaller one, so the panel keeps its earlier size.

Validation: `npm run verify` on the final candidate (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 318 unit/HTTP tests in 52 files, production build, 212 browser scenarios, all on the first run) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Log: `artifacts/composer-growth/verify.log`. The production CSS keeps the `field-sizing`, `lh` and `min()` declarations intact. `tests/composer-growth.spec.ts` checks three lines at rest and with a short draft, growth to four lines without scrolling, the desktop cap (a 450px composer box in a 900px window), the four-line phone cap, that the capped field keeps its last line in view, that usage details opened with a full composer stay between the conversation's top and the composer without intercepting the Model picker (with the previous bound the panel's top was at -14px), and the return to three lines. Its following scenario keeps the last answer paragraph fully visible while a 40-line draft grows the composer, returns to the same viewport height at the end when the draft is cleared, and leaves a scrolled-up reading position in place; without the viewport observer it fails with the end 319px out of view. Screenshots: `artifacts/composer-growth-desktop.png`, `artifacts/composer-growth-mobile.png`, `artifacts/composer-growth-usage-desktop.png`, `artifacts/composer-growth-usage-mobile.png`, `artifacts/composer-growth-following.png`. The native app was not rebuilt, since the change touches only style rules and one DOM observer in the same engine.

## Smooth chat scrolling and quicker disclosures — 2026-09-23

After the design system update, scrolling and moving the pointer over a long chat stuttered, and opening Work history or a group of tool calls lagged. Profiling the largest local conversation (34 messages, 537 tool calls, about 32,000 elements) in Edge 153, the Chromium build of the installed WebView2 runtime, at 2560×1300 and 150% scale showed that each style recalculation cost about 1.5 ms, even one that restyled a single hovered row. The drag-cursor rules of the sidebar and artifact dividers, `.app-shell:has(.sidebar-resizer.dragging) *` and its artifact twin, matched every element, and Chromium keeps `:has()` results only within one recalculation, so each recalculation searched the whole app shell again; the production build even merged each pair into one `:is()` selector. The rules predate the redesign, but its hover transitions on Work history rows, tool calls and summaries restyle an element on every animation frame. Sweeping the pointer over an open Work history took 18 recalculations and 75 ms of style work before the redesign and 477 recalculations and 741 ms after it.

Each divider now puts a class on the root element only while it is dragged (`sidebar-resizing`, `artifact-resizing`), and the cursor, text selection and preview iframe rules select `:root.<class> *`, which Chromium rejects per element without searching. The hover transitions stay as the design system specifies. The conversation's wheel listener, which only stops following when the reader scrolls up, is now passive: Svelte attached `onwheel` as a blocking listener, so the start of every scroll waited for the main thread.

With the fix, the same sweep takes 28–39 ms of style work and 375–440 ms of main-thread time instead of 460–800 ms and 800–1,400 ms, and wheel scrolling with the pointer over those rows drops from 210–290 ms to 19–23 ms of style work. Opening the largest Work history renders in 7 ms instead of 22 ms (style 2 ms instead of 16 ms), and its largest group of tool calls opens in 8.5 ms instead of 21 ms, with 1–2 ms of synchronous style and layout instead of 8–10 ms. The dev server build shows the same pattern (Work history in 11 ms instead of 22 ms). Opening the conversation spends 9 ms instead of 49 ms on style but still takes about 250 ms (310 ms in the dev build), nearly all of it Svelte rendering the collapsed Work histories, which stay in the DOM. Frame pacing in headless Edge varied too much on this 239 Hz machine to show the stutter itself reliably, so these figures compare main-thread work.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 318 unit/HTTP tests in 52 files, production build, 209 browser scenarios, all on the first run) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. Log: `artifacts/chat-performance/verify.log`. `src/lib/style-selectors.test.ts` rejects any stylesheet or component rule that pairs `:has()` with a universal selector; it fails against the previous dividers and names both rules. The divider scenarios in `workspace.spec.ts` and `work-features.spec.ts` now check that the window shows the resize cursor, and the artifact preview ignores the pointer, only while a drag lasts, including after Escape; they pass against both the previous and the new rules. DevTools listener inspection reports the conversation's wheel listener as passive. The profiling loaded the local workspace into the desktop mock and printed only timings and element counts; its scripts stayed in the session scratchpad. The native app was not rebuilt, since the change touches only style rules and one DOM listener in the same engine.

## Reasoning inside Work history, without duplicates — 2026-09-23

Claude Fable replies showed each thinking paragraph twice, in a Reasoning disclosure detached from the activity it explained. Claude Code prints every content block of a message as its own `assistant` line, and Fable messages open with a signature-only thinking block, so the visible thinking streamed at index 1 (`message:1`) while its snapshot line held it at array index 0. The decoder treated that array index as the block's position and saved a second copy as `message:0`; the reported workspace held 84 such pairs, all identical and adjacent. `ReasoningDecoder` now ignores snapshot thinking for a message whose thinking arrived as partial stream events, numbers unstreamed snapshots by position across the message's lines, and reserves none of the 64 items for empty thinking blocks (Fable's leading block used one per message).

Reasoning now appears where it happened: in the live activity and in Work history, between the progress comments and tool groups around it, as muted Markdown beside a brain mark that aligns with tool group labels. The separate disclosure is gone. `activityEntries` in `src/lib/activity-groups.ts` builds the timeline and shows identical text once per Claude message, which hides duplicates already saved without rewriting stored or synced history; run against the local workspace it showed 90 of 174 saved blocks, every distinct text once.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 317 unit/HTTP tests in 51 files, production build, 209 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 269 Rust tests with nine opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/reasoning-inline/{verify,rust-tests}.log`. The three new Rust tests (the recorded Fable stream-then-snapshot sequence, split snapshots without a stream, and empty blocks under the item bound) fail against the previous decoder. `tests/reasoning.spec.ts` checks the live and saved timeline order across completed, stopped and failed replies and reload, sanitizing, highlighting and truncation, and a relay-restored legacy duplicate shown once; `tests/chat-virtual-space.spec.ts` now collapses Work history and a tool group instead of the removed disclosure.

Live CLI check: three short Fable `stream-json` probes with Claude Code 2.1.278, run with the inherited `CLAUDE*` desktop variables removed, printed one `assistant` line per content block and opened thinking turns with a signature-only block. None of them produced visible thinking text, so the duplicate path itself is covered by the replayed transcript sequence rather than a live reply. The native app was not rerun for this change.

## Rewind and file Undo ported to development — 2026-09-23

A Codex session implemented Rewind and file Undo on 2026-09-19 (`codex/rewind-undo`, commit `5f282e9`) and passed its checks, but pushed only that branch; it was never merged, and its GitHub branch was later deleted. This port applies the feature to `development` and adapts it to what changed since: parked processes, account switching, compaction, the design system, and relay merging.

Native sessions: a request older than the host's saved conversation revision or native binding is rejected with one shared message, while a newer request can arrive before relay sync and proceeds. After a revision change the bound session is resumed only when the retained history still continues exactly what it received (Undo rewind). Otherwise the history counts as rewritten: the next reply starts a fresh session with a Work history note, an account switch never carries the rewound transcript to the new account, and `/compact` explains that the next reply starts a new session.

Frontend: any submission, including Retry, ends the Undo rewind window. On the branch, a retry after a rewind left the removed messages restorable after the retried reply, out of order. `/compact` is unavailable while a rewind can be undone. Both dialogs use the shared dialog shell and tokens, and checking files for Undo can be cancelled. **Rewind here** sits below your bubble, revealed on hover or keyboard focus and always shown on touch screens. Rewind, Undo edits and Retry have distinct icons, and the rewound notice is a neutral status. After a rewind or Undo rewind, focus returns to the composer and the chat shows its new end. Relay merging keeps a one-sided archive change from another device instead of creating a conflict copy. `scripts/native-rewind.tauri.json` now builds an isolated binary instead of running `tauri dev`.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 316 unit/HTTP tests in 51 files, production build, 209 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 266 Rust tests with nine opt-in cases ignored. The first full run timed out one unrelated scenario (`markdown.spec.ts`) before its page rendered; it passed alone and in the clean rerun. Ported coverage: `rewind.test.ts`, `tests/rewind.spec.ts`, the relay Undo job test, the transport receipt test, `undo.rs` and the file-change decoder. New coverage: Undo rewind resuming its session and a later file Undo starting fresh, no transfer or compaction after a rewind under another account, archive merging, and Retry ending the Undo rewind window.

Native Windows verification used the isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.rewind-qa` build (separate Cargo target, CDP 9593) launched with `npm run start:windows -- -Executable`, with real Claude (`sonnet`) and Codex (`gpt-5.6-sol`) terminal logins. `scripts/rewind-native-smoke.mjs` passed for both providers. It covered a tool-created file; Rewind with the draft kept; Undo rewind resuming the same native session, now checked by an extra reply; and a second Rewind whose next reply started a new session without the discarded codeword (binding revision 3). It also refused Undo while the file had a later manual edit, then removed the created file. `scripts/undo-native-smoke.mjs` passed a real Codex add/edit/delete: a conflicting file blocked Undo before any write, all three files were restored, the receipt survived reload, and a repeated request kept a later user edit. Reports and screenshots are under `artifacts/rewind/`. WSL, macOS/Linux, a second account on the same provider (account switch after a rewind is covered only by Rust tests), a paired Viewer and a physical phone were not exercised.

## Reply durations in minutes and seconds — 2026-09-23

The reply footer printed each reply's elapsed time as raw seconds (`1710.8s`), and total AI time kept tenths of a second after minutes and hours (`1h 7m 32.0s`). `formatReplyTime` in `src/lib/replies.ts` now keeps tenths only under a minute (`4.6s`) and rounds longer durations to whole seconds with minutes and, when needed, hours (`28m 31s`, `1h 7m 32s`); rounding that reaches a minute or an hour carries over (59.95 seconds shows `1m 0s`). `ReplyFooter.svelte` uses it for the elapsed time in the summary row and the expanded breakdown, and `NativeWorkflowPanel.svelte` for workflow run and agent durations. Stored `durationMs` values and the total calculation are unchanged, so saved history shows the new format without migration.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 305 unit/HTTP tests in 50 files, production build, 205 browser scenarios) and Rust formatting; Clippy and the Rust tests were not rerun because no Rust file changed. The first cold run in the fresh worktree timed out the sidebar update scenario on its first page load; it passed alone and in the full rerun. `replies.test.ts` covers the minute and hour boundaries and the durations from the report, and the total AI time scenario in `tests/workspace.spec.ts` now checks the elapsed text (`1m 1s` for a 61.25-second stopped reply) and whole-second totals. A temporary browser fixture rendered replies of 4.6, 1,710.8, 76 and 2,260.6 seconds as `4.6s`/`4.6s total`, `28m 31s`/`28m 35s total`, `1m 16s`/`29m 51s total` and `37m 41s`/`1h 7m 32s total`, with a workflow card showing `25m 0s` and an agent `12m 34s` (`artifacts/reply-time-format/after.png`). This display-only change needed no native build.

## Unsent drafts per chat and location — 2026-09-23

The composer held one draft that opening another chat or a new conversation cleared, and nothing survived a restart; only forks kept a session copy of the source chat's text. Drafts are now device-local and keyed per conversation, and per folder or Standalone location for new conversations (`src/lib/drafts.ts`). `+page.svelte` records the composer under its draft key, switches drafts when a chat or new-chat location opens, carries the text when a new chat's folder or computer changes or `/new` runs (a draft already at the destination stays first), and removes a draft when it is sent, steered, queued, cleared, or its conversation is deleted locally or through sync. Desktop saves text and mention identity in app-data `drafts.json` through asynchronous native commands (`src-tauri/src/drafts.rs`: validated, 2.5 MB bound, atomic replace); the Viewer uses workspace-scoped localStorage cleared with the rest of its cache and merges saves per conversation with other tabs. Saving is throttled to 300 ms while typing and flushed on blur, hide, pagehide, title-bar close and update restart. Following a notification now opens its chat directly instead of asking to finish a draft elsewhere; otherwise a restored draft would have blocked a cold-start deep link. Only an image still being read defers it.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 285 unit/HTTP tests in 49 files, production build, 205 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 257 Rust tests with nine opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/drafts/{verify,rust-tests,clippy}.log`. New coverage: `drafts.test.ts` (keys, saved form without images, lenient reading, limits, per-key merge across tabs, carrying into an existing draft), sign-out clearing in `browser-workspace.test.ts`, `tests/drafts.spec.ts` (per-chat drafts across switching, reloads, sending from History and deletion; separate folder and Standalone new-chat drafts, carrying on folder change and restart; mention identity after restart; the title-bar close saving first), and the Viewer case in `tests/private-workspaces.spec.ts` (restored after reload, never on the server, removed at sign-out). Updated scenarios cover notification deep links, template insertion across reload, and sidebar **+** actions that now reopen their location's draft.

`scripts/drafts-native-smoke.mjs` drove the isolated `com.vinicius.agentstudio.drafts-qa` build through the Task Scheduler launcher: drafts typed in a seeded chat and a Standalone new chat were written by an immediate title-bar close, restored after a relaunch, absent from `workspace.json`, and removed from `drafts.json` once cleared (`artifacts/drafts/native-result.json`, `native-restored.png`). No provider request was made. Closing from the taskbar blurs the window first, which saves; Alt+F4 relies on the throttle, so up to 300 ms of typing can be lost there. Attached images intentionally stay only for the session. WSL, macOS, Linux and a paired phone were not exercised natively.

## Chat content stays in place when expanding or collapsing — 2026-09-23

Collapsing a disclosure near the end of a chat shortened the conversation below the scroll position, so the browser clamped the scroll offset and pulled every message down. In the reported case, closing an expanded Reasoning block a few hundred pixels above the end moved its summary about 300px away from the pointer. Expanding content while following a running reply had the opposite problem: following changed only on scroll events, so the next streamed event scrolled to the new end and moved the expanded content away.

`src/lib/virtual-space.ts` captures activations of summaries, `aria-expanded` disclosures other than popup triggers, and tabs inside the conversation. It records the first visible content element and the activated control, then restores the first of them that survived in the next frame, before paint. A collapse that removes the visible content, such as **Show fewer files** read from the bottom of a long list, keeps the control in place instead. When the restored position lies past the new end, a blank spacer after the messages supplies exactly the missing space. Scroll events, content growth, and viewport resizes trim it to the blank gap below the real end, so scrolling up erases it and the scroll range never extends past the held position; conversation changes clear it. Toggles then update following like scroll events: content revealed past the end stops following, returning to the end resumes it, and while space is held the following state is kept, so streamed content fills the space before the chat follows.

`tests/chat-virtual-space.spec.ts` covers collapsing Reasoning near the end on desktop and at 390px (summary and scroll position unchanged, space equal to the visible gap, no further downward scrolling, partial and full erasure when scrolling up, the real end restored, and a new conversation clearing it), expanding and collapsing a tool group at the end of a running reply (following resumes, then an expanded group is not scrolled away), collapsing while following (a short answer fills the space in place and longer output is followed), and Show all/fewer files with a later exchange below. Before the change, the summary moved 253px (desktop) and 189px (mobile), the next event after expanding scrolled 1513px, collapsing while following moved the summary 349px, and Show fewer files moved its button 1100px out of view. All pass after it, and ad hoc runs also pass at 125% and 150% device scale factors. Evidence: `artifacts/chat-virtual-space-{desktop,mobile}.png`.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 279 unit/HTTP tests in 48 files, production build, 200 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and 254 Rust tests with nine opt-in cases ignored. Logs: `artifacts/virtual-space-{verify,rust}.log`. The behavior is DOM-only and the browser checks use Edge, whose Chromium engine WebView2 shares; a native `tauri dev` session and WebKit for the iOS Viewer were not run.

## Claude reply costs count each reply once — 2026-09-22

Claude's `total_cost_usd` and `modelUsage` are running totals, while `usage` and its `output_tokens_details.thinking_tokens` cover one turn. Probes with Claude Code 2.1.278 and Haiku showed the total continuing within one stream-json process (0.0283 → 0.0393) and across `/compact` (0.0062 → 0.0126 → 0.0286). Separate `claude -p` processes that exited cleanly also carried it into a resumed session (0.0228 → 0.0260 → 0.0292), but Agent Studio terminates parked processes, and a process resuming after that restarted from zero. Agent Studio saved the running total as each reply's cost and summed replies, so costs on a reused process were overstated and chat totals grew with every reply; reasoning counts were cumulative too. Each parked process now remembers its last reported total, a reply records the change from it (a fresh process starts at zero; a dropping total stays unknown), reasoning sums per-turn counts, and the readings carry `scope: "reply"`. The chat total excludes older saved running totals and says so; the reply footer labels them **Chat cost so far**. The opt-in `installed_claude_reply_costs_follow_each_process_running_total` test drove the production runner with Haiku: replies on a new process, the same parked process, and a fresh process resuming the session recorded $0.0242, $0.0053 and $0.0045 for running totals of $0.0242, $0.0296 and $0.0045, where the old code would have saved $0.0242, $0.0296 and $0.0045 and summed them. An earlier design kept a per-session total next to the binding; the real run showed the resumed process restarting at zero, so it was replaced by per-process accounting.

The same work fixed a regression that multi-turn replies expose: the follow-up turn's `system/init` rebound the native session with `received: false`, so after a reply that waited for background work (or a workflow), the next message resent the previous one as an interrupted delivery and refreshed the instructions. A follow-up init now keeps the reply's confirmed state; the updated runner test fails without the fix.

Validation: `npm run verify` (zero Svelte/TypeScript diagnostics, 279 unit/HTTP tests, production build, 195 browser scenarios), Rust formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and the full Rust suite (254 tests, nine opt-in cases ignored). A first browser run failed four unrelated scenarios after source edits during the run reloaded their pages through Vite; the clean rerun passed. New coverage for running-total subtraction, dropping totals, and per-turn reasoning in the decoder; the controlled CLI reporting cumulative totals over a multi-turn reply, the next reply on the same parked process, and a reply after an interrupted one; spend sums and older-history exclusion; and a browser scenario for the footer label and chat total.

## Claude chat instructions in Settings — 2026-09-22

Settings now has **Claude chat instructions**: one workspace-wide text appended to the system prompt of every interactive Claude chat, pre-filled with a default that asks Claude to run tests, builds and type checks in the foreground and to background only servers, watchers and apps left for the user. The text is editable, can be cleared or reset to the default, syncs as the optional `claudeInstructions` workspace field, and reaches the CLI as one literal `--append-system-prompt` argument that also participates in parked-process identity. See docs/NATIVE-SESSIONS.md.

New coverage: the default, off and stored forms, the 4,000-character and null-character bounds, workspace/export/relay schema round trips, and sync merges (edits on either side, reset, off, concurrent edits); Rust validation for Claude-only interactive requests, the literal argument with quotes and newlines, omission of blank text and background requests, stdin exclusion, and the process fingerprint; and a browser scenario covering the default text, save, reload, a 390px layout, a Codex request without the field, Claude requests with custom and default text, reset, and turning it off. A Claude Code 2.1.278 stream-json probe with Haiku confirmed that a multi-line `--append-system-prompt` value with quotes and a backslash is honored.
